• The posterior insula, section 2.1.6, the primary interoceptive cortex, a map of the body's interior, re-represented in the anterior insula, section 2.2, where body state becomes subjective feeling, with the anterior cingulate and ventromedial prefrontal cortex as its motivational and decisional clients.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The vagus nerve is the trunk line, roughly 80 percent afferent, cargo of mechanical and chemical organ state formatted as slow unmyelinated and thin myelinated traffic into the solitary nucleus.

Spinal visceral afferents travel with sympathetic nerves into the dorsal horn and ascend with the spinothalamic system, carrying visceral pain and temperature, in the corpus's reading a homeostatic afferent channel of its own.

Solitary-to-parabrachial and parabrachial-to-thalamic tracts complete the ascent, a posterior-to-anterior insular interface converts map into feeling, and blood-borne signals enter through sensory circumventricular organs, windows where the brain tastes its own plasma, making this the one sensory system with a hormonal input port.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the receptors the representation is raw physiological variables, pressures, gas tensions, stretches, temperatures, cytokine levels.

At the solitary nucleus it becomes organ-labelled state channels already driving reflexes, and at the parabrachial nucleus it becomes prioritised need signals, hunger, thirst, air hunger, nausea, ranked for urgency.

At the posterior insula it becomes a conscious-capable map of the body's interior, at the anterior insula it becomes feeling, an integrated, valenced summary, this-body-now, that correlates with subjective intensity rather than raw input, and at the cingulate and ventromedial prefrontal interfaces it becomes motivation and evidence, the somatic weight on the scales of choice.

The chain's signature transformation is thus measurement to feeling, physiology entering as numbers and leaving as how it is to be you.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

The solitary nucleus runs the reflex-arbiter and set-point comparator motifs of the Layer 8 catalogue, the parabrachial nucleus runs the catalogue's alarm-broadcast motif, and the insular chain runs canonical columns arranged as the catalogue's re-representation cascade, each stage adding context to the last.

The anterior insula holds the von Economo neuron, the large fast projection cell the Layer 6 and 7 volumes flag as this system's specialised hardware.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Baroreceptor and chemoreceptor loops run beat to beat and breath to breath, under a second, while gut chemistry and hormonal ports report over minutes and immune signalling over hours.

Heartbeat-evoked potentials show cortical registration of each systole within about 200 to 400 milliseconds, and the felt background updates on a scale of seconds to minutes, the slow weather against vision's fast news.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The vagal trunk carries on the order of 100,000 fibres, most of the viscera's vast surface reports without any conscious address, and localisation is poor, visceral pain famously referred to the body wall.

Conscious access is a narrow window, in heartbeat-detection tasks only a minority of people perform accurately, with wide individual differences in interoceptive accuracy that predict emotional granularity and decision quality, moderate confidence.

MODULATION AND GATING:

Attention gates access sharply, the same visceral input felt or unfelt by insular gain, and anxiety raises the gain pathologically.

The parabrachial alarm channel overrides all gating for air hunger and severe visceral threat, neuromodulators retune the chain with state, and inflammation opens the gain wide, producing the heavy-bodied feeling of sickness behaviour.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

Set points and expected ranges are learned, the system predicting its body and feeling the errors, and interoceptive predictions are retrained by experience, for better in contemplative training, which measurably sharpens accuracy and thickens anterior insula, and for worse in panic disorder, where benign signals are learned as catastrophic.

Conditioned nausea and conditioned immune responses show the chain's associative reach.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The brainstem loops run from before birth, while insular re-representation and interoceptive accuracy mature through childhood alongside emotional vocabulary.

Adolescent remodelling of the insular-cingulate axis coincides with the peak onset ages of anxiety, an actively studied coincidence.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Homeostatic afference is as old as circulation, and the solitary-parabrachial core is standard vertebrate equipment.

The lamina-one spinothalamic channel and its dedicated thalamo-insular relay are treated in the corpus as a primate elaboration, and the anterior insular re-representation with von Economo neurons, shared with great apes, elephants, and cetaceans, is the seat the framework assigns to feeling as such.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

This system is the endocrine axes' sensory organ, its circumventricular ports reading leptin, ghrelin, insulin, angiotensin, cortisol, and inflammatory cytokines directly from blood.

Its outputs close the great loops, solitary and parabrachial signals steering the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, fluid balance, and appetite, so that every axis of Part Six runs through its readings.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake, the chain publishes the feeling background and weights ongoing choice.

In slow-wave sleep the reflex floors keep running while insular access closes, blood pressure and heart rate dipping under uncontested brainstem control, and visceral alarm, a full bladder, air hunger, can still force waking.

In rapid-eye-movement (REM) sleep autonomic state turns stormy, and under stress the system's reports both drive and are driven by sympathetic arousal, the loop that lets anxiety feel like heart disease.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Panic disorder is the system's false-alarm disease, catastrophic misreading of its own signals, and depression reliably shows blunted and mispredicted interoception with insular involvement.

Autonomic neuropathy in diabetes silences afferents, hiding cardiac ischaemia, pure autonomic failure and multiple system atrophy break the reflex floors, eating disorders involve degraded hunger and satiety readings, and insular stroke can distort bodily feeling up to somatoparaphrenia's disowned limbs.

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system is the felt tone of existence, the difference between days that feel light and days that feel leaden, before any reason is found.

The corpus's wager, following the somatic marker line, is that no purely disembodied intelligence will feel anything, because feeling is this chain's product, a body reported inward.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Mechanical, chemical, thermal, and immune state from every organ over about 100,000 vagal fibres plus spinal lines.

◦ Blood-borne hormones at the circumventricular ports; attention and expectation from cortical clients.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Reflex corrections to autonomic effectors within a heartbeat.

◦ Need states, hunger, thirst, air hunger, to hypothalamic drives, and the integrated feeling state to insula, cingulate, and ventromedial prefrontal decision circuitry.

• STATE.

◦ The current gravito-metabolic body model, prediction-error levels, insular gain, inflammation flag.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Vagus about 80 percent afferent; heartbeat detection accurate in a minority of untrained people; visceral localisation coarse to the point of referral.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Baroreflex under 1 second; heartbeat-evoked cortical response 200 to 400 milliseconds; hormonal ports minutes; immune afference hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Panic disorder as false alarm; diabetic autonomic neuropathy and silent ischaemia as lost signal; multiple system atrophy at the reflex core; depression's blunted body reading.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As an engineering pack this system is the body_state_estimation_service, modules visceral_sensor_bus, solitary_reflex_floor, parabrachial_alarm_broadcaster, and insular_feeling_integrator, subscribing to organ telemetry and blood chemistry and publishing a low-frequency feeling_state topic that the emotion, memory, and decision packs all import.

The engineering analogue is a health-monitoring and observability service with alerting, the biological difference being that its dashboard is not read by the system's operators, it is the operators' mood.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn body-state evidence into a feeling signal that tilts a decision between two options.

Create a number variable called heart rate error with the difference between measured and predicted heart rate.

Create a number variable called breath error with the difference between measured and predicted breathing.

Create a number variable called gut error with the difference between measured and predicted gut state.

Calculate the weighted sum of heart rate error, breath error, and gut error using the insular gain for each.

Set the feeling signal to the result, where positive means unease and negative means comfort.

Create a number variable called risky option value with the expected payoff of the risky option.

Create a number variable called safe option value with the expected payoff of the safe option.

Subtract the product of the feeling signal and the somatic weight from the risky option value.

Set the adjusted risky value to the result.

If the adjusted risky value is greater than the safe option value is true, then choose the risky option.

Otherwise, choose the safe option.

Display the feeling signal and the choice to the console.

The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the somatic-marker idea is one weighted sum feeding one comparison, and the state-dependence of choice shows in four printed lines.

# Body-state evidence feeding a decision: interoceptive prediction
# errors are integrated into a feeling signal (anterior insula), which
# weights a risky choice (Damasio's somatic marker hypothesis;
# Bechara et al. 1997; Craig, Nat Rev Neurosci 2009).
INSULAR_GAIN = {"heart": 0.5, "breath": 0.3, "gut": 0.2}
SOMATIC_WEIGHT = 2.0

def feeling(measured, predicted):
    """Weighted interoceptive prediction error: + is unease, - is comfort."""
    return sum(INSULAR_GAIN[k] * (measured[k] - predicted[k]) for k in measured)

def choose(risky_value, safe_value, feel):
    adjusted = risky_value - SOMATIC_WEIGHT * max(feel, 0.0)
    return ("risky" if adjusted > safe_value else "safe"), round(adjusted, 2)

predicted = {"heart": 70, "breath": 12, "gut": 0}
for label, measured in [("calm body   ", {"heart": 70, "breath": 12, "gut": 0}),
                        ("racing heart", {"heart": 78, "breath": 16, "gut": 2})]:
    f = feeling(measured, predicted)
    pick, adj = choose(risky_value=6.0, safe_value=4.0, feel=f)
    print(f"{label}: feeling={f:+.1f}  adjusted risky value={adj}  -> {pick}")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The only sensory system aimed at its owner, entering through nerves and bloodstream alike, whose finished product is not a percept of anything but the feeling of being someone.


PART TWO. THE MOTOR AND ACTION SYSTEMS

Everything the previous part sensed exists so that this part can act.

A brain that only perceived would be a very expensive weather station, and evolution does not pay for weather stations.

The systems gathered here turn intention into movement: a direct command line to the muscles, a machine for choosing which movement to release, a calibration engine that keeps movement accurate, a specialist rig for aiming the eyes, and a quiet outflow that steers the organs no one chooses to move.

Four of the five are tagged non-cognitive because they execute, relay, or regulate rather than deliberate; the fifth, the selection loop system, earns its cognitive tag because choosing is a computation and its associative and limbic channels are where thought becomes deed.

Read them as one story with a single moral: an action is not a single event but a committee decision, drafted in cortex, vetoed or approved in the basal ganglia, proof-read by the cerebellum, and finally signed by a motoneuron.


CHAPTER 8. THE CORTICOSPINAL SYSTEM (Formal descriptor: pyramidal motor pathway) (Plain-English Coined Name: The Straight-Line Command Cable) (NON-COG)

Lift a finger and you have just used the newest, straightest wire in the vertebrate body: a single axon that can run from the crown of your head to the base of your spine without changing trains.

Most motor commands in the animal kingdom are relayed through the brainstem like a message passed hand to hand; the primate corticospinal system adds an express lane that, in its fastest fibres, touches the motoneuron directly.

That directness is what buys the independent finger movements a human hand can make and a cat's paw cannot.

Straight-Line names the system's signature: the monosynaptic shortcut from cortex to motoneuron that skips the usual relays.

Command names its content: descending motor instruction, not sensation returning.

Cable names its form: a dense bundle of roughly a million axons, packed and partly insulated, running the length of the neuraxis.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The corticospinal system, also called the pyramidal tract because it passes through the pyramid-shaped ridges on the front of the medulla (the lowest part of the brainstem), is the direct descending pathway that carries voluntary movement commands from the cerebral cortex to the motoneurons and interneurons of the spinal cord.

Its headline accomplishment is fractionated movement: the ability to move one digit without moving its neighbours, which is the anatomical basis of manual dexterity, tool use, and skilled hand-and-finger control.

It does not by itself decide what to do or whether the movement was accurate; it is the effector cable that transmits a decision made elsewhere, which is why it is tagged non-cognitive.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The primary motor cortex, the strip of frontal cortex just ahead of the central groove, is the main source of the tract and holds the map of the body from which commands are issued.

• The premotor and supplementary motor areas, just forward of the primary strip, contribute a large share of fibres and supply the postural and sequencing context for a movement.

• The primary somatosensory and parietal cortices contribute descending fibres too, many of which end on sensory relay neurons in the cord and adjust the touch and position signals coming back up.

• The posterior limb of the internal capsule, the white-matter funnel deep in the hemisphere, is where the widely spread cortical fibres gather into a compact bundle.

• The cerebral peduncle, pons, and medullary pyramid carry the bundle down through the brainstem.

• The pyramidal decussation, at the junction of medulla and spinal cord, is where roughly 85 to 90 per cent of fibres cross to the opposite side to form the lateral corticospinal tract, the rest continuing uncrossed as the anterior tract.

• The spinal grey matter, especially the ventral horn lamina where motoneurons sit, is the terminal station where the command meets the final common path to the muscle.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The pathway is one long tract with named segments, but its interfaces are worth stating in cargo-and-format terms.

• Origin: layer 5 pyramidal cells across motor and sensory cortex; cargo: a rate-coded and population-coded movement command; format: trains of action potentials.

• The internal capsule interface concentrates a sheet of cortex into a cable a few millimetres wide, which is why a small stroke there can paralyse a whole half of the body.

• The decussation interface swaps sides, so the left hemisphere commands the right hand.

• The spinal interface is the critical hand-off: fast fibres synapse directly onto alpha motoneurons (monosynaptic, the primate express lane), while most fibres synapse onto spinal interneurons that then drive motoneurons, giving the cord room to shape the raw command.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the cortex the representation is abstract and high-level: a population of neurons encodes an intended direction, force, and speed of movement, not the twitch of any single muscle.

Descending the tract the signal is not transformed much in content; the tract is a faithful conduit, and this fidelity is the point.

At the spinal interface the transformation happens: the abstract command is fanned out onto motoneuron pools and interneuron networks, combined with sensory feedback and spinal reflex circuitry, and converted into the specific pattern of muscle activation that produces the movement.

The monosynaptic component is the sharpest transformation of all, because it lets cortex speak almost a muscle's language directly, bypassing the cord's own committee.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

At the cortical source it runs the deep-layer output-projection motif, in which large pyramidal cells integrate local and long-range input and emit a single descending decision.

Along the tract it runs the labelled-line relay motif, a private one-to-few wiring that preserves the identity of the signal.

At the cord it runs the divergence-and-local-inhibition motif, where one descending fibre contacts many spinal cells and recruits inhibitory interneurons to sculpt reciprocal muscle control (contract one, relax its opposite).

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

The system is fast and phasic.

The fastest fibres conduct at up to about 70 metres per second, with an average around 60 metres per second, so a command reaches the lumbar cord in under 20 milliseconds.

Activity is tied to the movement: high-conduction-velocity fibres fire in bursts during rapid movements, while lower-velocity fibres discharge tonically during slow, sustained ones.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The human tract holds roughly one million axons per side.

Its fibre-diameter spectrum has two peaks, near 1 micrometre and near 7 micrometres (a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre), showing a mix of thin and thick fibres.

The giant Betz cells of the motor cortex, some with cell bodies 100 micrometres or more across, contribute only about 1 to 3 per cent of the fibres, so the tract is mostly medium and small axons, not giants.

The limit that matters clinically is that this million-fibre cable has almost no redundancy at the internal capsule, so a lesion a few millimetres wide can abolish voluntary movement of half the body.

MODULATION AND GATING:

The tract itself is a cable and is modulated mainly at its two ends.

At the cortical source, dopamine, noradrenaline, and acetylcholine tune the excitability of the output cells and the readiness to move.

At the spinal end, descending serotonergic and noradrenergic fibres from the brainstem set the gain of motoneurons and reflex arcs, effectively deciding how loud the command sounds when it arrives.

The chief gate sits upstream, in the basal ganglia selection loops of the next chapter, which decide whether a cortical command is released at all.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

The cortical origin is highly plastic: the motor map reorganises with practice, and skilled training expands the territory devoted to the trained muscles.

The synapses of the tract strengthen and weaken with use, and recovery after stroke depends heavily on surviving corticospinal fibres re-establishing spinal connections.

The tract is not a fixed wire but a trainable one, which is why rehabilitation works at all.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Corticospinal axons grow down during late fetal life and reach the lower cord around birth.

Their myelination (the fatty insulation that speeds conduction) continues for the first two years and beyond, which is one reason a newborn cannot perform skilled finger movements.

An early over-production of fibres is followed by pruning, and the monosynaptic cortex-to-motoneuron connections mature especially late, tracking the slow arrival of manual dexterity in childhood.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The corticospinal tract is a mammalian invention, small in rodents and progressively elaborated in primates.

The direct monosynaptic cortex-to-motoneuron connection is largely a primate feature and is richest in humans and other dexterous primates, which is the current best explanation for our unmatched hand control.

In evolutionary terms this is a recent express lane laid over an ancient brainstem road network.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

Thyroid hormone is required for normal myelination and for the timely maturation of the tract, so developmental thyroid deficiency impairs motor milestones.

Cortisol and other stress hormones can transiently alter cortical excitability and motor performance, but the tract has no dedicated hormone that it secretes.

Its endocrine story is one of dependence on metabolic and developmental hormones rather than participation in an axis.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake, the system is available and gated by intention.

In slow-wave sleep, descending drive is low and voluntary movement is suppressed centrally.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep (the dreaming state), the cortical output cells can be highly active but the command is blocked at the spinal end by an active brainstem-driven muscle paralysis (atonia), which is why we do not act out dreams; failure of that block is the disorder of dream-enactment.

Under stress, the tract's gain rises and fine control can degrade into tremor and clumsiness.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Damage to the tract produces the upper-motor-neuron syndrome: weakness, increased muscle tone (spasticity), brisk reflexes, and an upgoing big toe on sole-stroking (the Babinski sign).

A capsular stroke causes contralateral hemiplegia (paralysis of the opposite half of the body).

Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis degrades the corticospinal cells together with the spinal motoneurons, combining upper- and lower-motor-neuron signs.

Hereditary spastic paraplegia is a length-dependent degeneration of the longest corticospinal fibres, causing progressive leg stiffness.

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system is the reason a human can thread a needle, sign a name, play a violin, and gesture while speaking.

Remove it and the body is not senseless but helpless in the fine grain: gross movements driven by brainstem pathways survive, but the skilled, individuated hand is gone.

It colours the organism as the author of dexterity.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Released movement commands from the basal-ganglia-thalamo-cortical selection loops; postural and timing context from premotor and supplementary areas; sensory feedback via parietal cortex; calibration corrections from the cerebellum via thalamus.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Direct excitation and interneuron-mediated drive to spinal alpha motoneurons; adjustment signals to spinal sensory relay neurons; conduction latency to lumbar cord under 20 milliseconds.

• STATE.

◦ Cortical output-cell excitability, tract firing pattern (phasic versus tonic), spinal motoneuron gain set by descending monoamines.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ About 1 million axons per side; conduction up to about 70 metres per second, mean near 60; roughly 85 to 90 per cent of fibres crossed at the decussation; Betz cells 1 to 3 per cent of fibres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Command transit head-to-lumbar under 20 milliseconds; myelination maturation over the first two years and beyond; map reorganisation over days to weeks of practice.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Capsular stroke gives contralateral hemiplegia; amyotrophic lateral sclerosis gives combined upper- and lower-motor-neuron loss; hereditary spastic paraplegia gives length-dependent leg spasticity.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The corticospinal system maps to a engineering module named motor_command_pipeline.

It subscribes to selected action plans from the selection loops and publishes effector commands with predicted sensory consequences.

The nearest engineering analogue is an actuator driver with feedforward compensation - the biological difference is that the driver itself is plastic, recalibrated continuously by the cerebellar system.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A million-fibre cable that crosses the midline at the medulla and, uniquely among descending pathways, reaches down and touches the motoneuron directly, so that a single stroke in a thumb-sized patch of internal capsule can paralyse half a body.


CHAPTER 9. THE CORTICO-BASAL-GANGLIA-THALAMO-CORTICAL LOOP SYSTEM (Formal descriptor: parallel re-entrant selection loops) (Plain-English Coined Name: The Choosing Machine) (COG)

Every instant, dozens of possible actions crowd the cortex at once: reach, speak, look, wait.

Something must pick one and suppress the rest, and that something is a set of loops that leave the cortex, pass through the deep grey nuclei of the basal ganglia, cross the thalamus, and return to the cortex they came from.

The trick of the machine is that its resting state is a brake: the basal ganglia continuously inhibit movement, and to act is to release the brake on exactly one channel while pressing harder on the others.

Choosing names the function: the resolution of competing candidate actions into a single released one.

Machine names its mechanical, re-entrant character: the same loop shape runs for motor acts, eye movements, thoughts, and desires.

Loop names its topology: output returns to origin, so the system is a control circuit, not a one-way pipe.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The cortico-basal-ganglia-thalamo-cortical loop system is a family of parallel re-entrant circuits, described in their modern form by Alexander, DeLong, and Strick in 1986, that link the cerebral cortex to the striatum, then to the pallidum and substantia nigra, then to the thalamus, and back to the cortex.

Its accomplishment is action selection: choosing which of many competing candidate behaviours or thoughts to release and which to suppress, biased by learned value signalled by dopamine.

Because its associative and limbic channels select thoughts, goals, and motivations rather than only muscle movements, the system is tagged cognitive.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The cerebral cortex, broadly, is both source and destination; each functional territory contributes to its own loop, so motor cortex feeds the motor loop and prefrontal cortex feeds the associative loop.

• The striatum, the input nucleus of the basal ganglia, is where cortical candidates land on medium spiny neurons and compete; its dopamine supply sets the terms of the competition.

• The external pallidum and the subthalamic nucleus form the indirect and hyperdirect control arms that raise or globally spread the brake.

• The internal pallidum and the substantia nigra pars reticulata are the output nuclei, tonically active and tonically inhibiting the thalamus, so their job is to remove inhibition from the chosen channel.

• The motor, associative, and limbic thalamic relay nuclei carry the released signal back up.

• The substantia nigra pars compacta and the ventral tegmental area supply the dopamine that teaches and biases the striatum.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The loops run on three canonical routes with distinct cargo and sign.

• The direct pathway: cortex excites striatum, striatum inhibits the output nuclei, the output nuclei stop inhibiting the thalamus, and the thalamus excites cortex; net effect, release the chosen action (a double negative that says go).

• The indirect pathway: cortex to striatum to external pallidum to subthalamic nucleus to output nuclei, which increases inhibition of the thalamus; net effect, suppress competing actions (say no).

• The hyperdirect pathway: cortex to subthalamic nucleus to output nuclei, a fast global brake that buys time before any choice is released.

• The teaching interface: dopamine cells project to the striatum and deliver a reward-prediction-error signal, format being a phasic rise for better-than-expected and a dip for worse-than-expected outcomes, which strengthens or weakens the cortical inputs that were just active.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the cortex the representation is a set of competing candidates, each a pattern of activity arguing for a different action or thought, none yet decisive.

In the striatum the candidates are re-weighted by learned value: dopamine makes the direct-pathway cells more responsive to strong inputs and the indirect-pathway cells less so, tilting the contest toward the option with the best history.

At the output nuclei the transformation is a winner-take-most: tonic inhibition is lifted from the single winning channel and maintained or deepened on the losers.

Back at the cortex the representation is now a decision, one channel disinhibited and freed to drive behaviour.

The central intellectual content is that the system converts a parallel field of possibilities into a single committed act, and it does so by selectively releasing a brake rather than by adding a push.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

It runs the tonic-inhibition-and-disinhibition motif, in which the default is off and selection is achieved by removing suppression.

It runs the lateral-competition motif in the striatum, where active candidates inhibit their neighbours.

It runs the reward-prediction-error teaching motif at the dopamine interface, a three-factor learning rule in which a synapse changes only when presynaptic activity, postsynaptic activity, and a dopamine signal coincide.

It runs the fast-global-brake motif in the hyperdirect pathway, a hold signal that prevents premature commitment.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Selection unfolds over tens to a few hundred milliseconds, slower than the corticospinal cable because it is a deliberation, not a relay.

The hyperdirect brake arrives first, within about 10 milliseconds of cortical activity; the direct release follows; the indirect suppression settles the losers.

Dopamine teaching signals are phasic bursts and dips lasting a fraction of a second, time-locked to outcomes, riding on a slow tonic dopamine background that sets overall vigour.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The human striatum contains on the order of 100 million or more medium spiny neurons, funnelling from a cortex of billions of cells into pallidal output nuclei of only a few hundred thousand, an enormous convergence.

The system can hold and arbitrate multiple parallel loops at once but can commit only a small number of channels to release simultaneously, which is a hard limit that shows as the difficulty of doing two chosen actions at the same time.

Dopamine cells number only a few hundred thousand yet address the whole striatum, so their teaching signal is broadcast, not private.

MODULATION AND GATING:

Dopamine is the master modulator, setting the balance between the go and no-go pathways and the vigour of chosen movements.

Acetylcholine from striatal interneurons opposes and shapes dopamine's action.

Serotonin and noradrenaline adjust the patience and gain of the loops.

The gate of the whole system is the output nuclei: their tonic inhibition is the brake, and every other input works by biasing whether that brake lifts.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

The striatal synapse is the site of reinforcement learning: corticostriatal connections strengthen when their activity is followed by a dopamine burst and weaken when followed by a dip.

Over many repetitions this turns a deliberate, cortically driven choice into an automatic habit run largely within the loop, which is the neural form of practice making perfect.

The same rule, applied to the limbic loop, teaches which goals and cues are worth pursuing.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The striatum forms from the ganglionic eminences of the embryonic forebrain, and its dopamine supply arrives and matures over the first two decades.

The dramatic remodelling of these loops in adolescence, with limbic channels maturing before the prefrontal control channels, is a leading account of adolescent impulsivity and reward-seeking.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The basal ganglia are an ancient vertebrate structure with the same selection architecture in fish, birds, and mammals, which strongly suggests that choosing among actions is one of the oldest jobs the forebrain evolved to do.

What primates and humans add is the expansion of the associative and limbic loops, extending an ancient movement-selector into a selector of thoughts and plans.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

Gonadal and stress hormones modulate dopamine signalling, so oestrogen influences striatal dopamine and reward learning across the menstrual cycle, and cortisol under stress shifts the balance from goal-directed toward habitual selection.

The system does not secrete a hormone of its own; its coupling is receptive, tuning its learning to the body's hormonal state.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake, the loops actively select behaviour and thought.

In slow-wave sleep, selection of overt action is suppressed but the loops participate in the offline replay that consolidates procedural memory.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep, dopamine and limbic activity are high while motor output is blocked, so the machine chooses freely in dreams without releasing movement.

Under stress, tonic dopamine and cortisol push selection toward fast, habitual, low-deliberation choices.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Parkinson's disease is loss of the dopamine cells, which unbalances the loops toward the brake and produces poverty and slowness of movement, rigidity, and tremor.

Huntington's disease first destroys the indirect-pathway striatal cells, releasing unwanted movements as chorea (involuntary dance-like jerks).

Obsessive-compulsive disorder, Tourette syndrome, and addiction are increasingly read as disorders of selection and value-learning in the associative and limbic loops.

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system gives the organism agency: the felt experience of deciding, initiating, and pursuing.

Its motor loop colours movement with vigour and readiness, its limbic loop colours life with wanting and drive, and its associative loop lets us choose thoughts as we choose acts.

Tip it toward the brake and a person becomes frozen and apathetic; tip it toward release and they become restless, impulsive, and driven.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Candidate action and thought patterns from all cortical territories; reward and outcome information via dopamine cells; contextual and salience signals from limbic structures; a global-stop demand via the hyperdirect pathway.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Selective disinhibition of one thalamocortical channel (go) and deepened inhibition of competitors (no-go); a vigour setting for the released action; an updated set of learned action values.

• STATE.

◦ Per-channel competition level in the striatum; tonic output-nucleus inhibition; tonic and phasic dopamine level; go versus no-go balance.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Roughly 100 million or more striatal medium spiny neurons; a few hundred thousand dopamine cells; three canonical pathways (direct, indirect, hyperdirect); four broad parallel loops (motor, oculomotor, associative, limbic).

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Hyperdirect brake about 10 milliseconds; full selection tens to hundreds of milliseconds; dopamine teaching bursts under a second; habit formation over many repetitions across days to months.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Parkinson's disease (brake too strong, movement lost); Huntington's disease (brake too weak, chorea); addiction, obsessive-compulsive disorder, and Tourette syndrome (mis-set value and selection in limbic and associative loops).

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline this system is the action_selection_loop_system.

It subscribes to a topic of candidate actions published by every cortical module, each candidate carrying a salience and a learned value, and it publishes a single release decision back to the originating cortical module together with a vigour parameter.

Its nearest engineering analogue is a priority arbiter combined with a reinforcement-learning value function: many requesters compete for a resource, a learned score breaks the tie, and a default-deny gate is opened for exactly the winner.

The biological difference is that the arbiter's default is inhibition released rather than permission granted, that the value function is taught by a single broadcast dopamine signal rather than a private reward wire per channel, and that the same circuit is duplicated in parallel for motor, oculomotor, associative, and limbic content.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to select one action from several competing candidates by combining each candidate's cortical salience with its dopamine-biased learned value, releasing the winner and suppressing the rest, across the motor, associative, and limbic channels.

Create a list called candidates, where each candidate has a name, a channel, a salience between zero and one, and a learned value between zero and one.

Create a number variable called dopamine with initial value 0.5, representing the current dopamine level that scales how much learned value counts.

Create an empty list called scores.

For each candidate in candidates, perform the following actions.

    Calculate the product of the candidate's learned value and dopamine.

    Calculate the sum of the candidate's salience and that product.

    Store the result as the candidate's selection score in scores.

End the loop.

Find the candidate whose selection score is the highest.

Set that candidate's decision to released.

For every other candidate, set its decision to suppressed.

Display each candidate's name, channel, score, and decision to the console.

The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the point is a competition among candidates settled by a dopamine-scaled value, and a short loop with a max over a list shows that arbitration with no ceremony.

# Parallel cortico-basal-ganglia-thalamo-cortical selection across
# motor, associative, and limbic channels. Learned value is weighted by
# dopamine; the default is inhibition, and only the winner is released.
# (Alexander, DeLong & Strick, Annu. Rev. Neurosci. 1986.)

dopamine = 0.7   # 0 = brake dominant (parkinsonian), 1 = strongly biased by value

candidates = [
    # name,           channel,        salience, learned_value
    ("reach_for_cup",  "motor",        0.40, 0.30),
    ("look_at_door",   "oculomotor",   0.55, 0.20),
    ("plan_the_route", "associative",  0.35, 0.75),
    ("crave_snack",    "limbic",       0.30, 0.90),
]

def score(salience, value, da):
    return salience + value * da        # double-negative "go": bias, don't push

scored = [(name, ch, score(s, v, dopamine)) for (name, ch, s, v) in candidates]
winner = max(scored, key=lambda c: c[2])

print(f"{'candidate':16}{'channel':13}{'score':>6}  decision")
for name, ch, sc in scored:
    decision = "RELEASED" if (name, ch, sc) == winner else "suppressed"
    print(f"{name:16}{ch:13}{sc:6.2f}  {decision}")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A set of parallel loops whose resting output is a brake on all action, which selects by lifting inhibition from exactly one channel, and whose choices are taught by a single broadcast dopamine signal that turns Parkinson's frozen brake and Huntington's leaky brake into opposite faces of one machine.


CHAPTER 10. THE CEREBELLAR SYSTEM (Formal descriptor: cortico-ponto-cerebello-thalamo-cortical calibration circuit) (Plain-English Coined Name: The Error-Correcting Rehearsal Engine) (NON-COG)

Behind the brainstem sits a structure the size of a fist that holds more than half of all the neurons in the brain, packed in a crystalline, repeating array.

It does not command movement and it does not choose it; it predicts what a movement will feel like, compares the prediction with what actually happens, and files the difference so the next attempt is a little better.

That is the whole trick, run in parallel across every motor stream and, in its newest parts, across several streams of thought.

Error-Correcting names its learning rule: it is taught by mistakes, delivered by a special error wire.

Rehearsal names its predictive job: it runs a fast internal model of the body ahead of the real thing.

Engine names its uniform, mass-produced circuitry: one microcircuit repeated millions of times.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The cerebellar system is the loop that carries a copy of every motor command, and much sensory context, from the cortex through the pons into the cerebellum, and returns a correction through the deep cerebellar nuclei and thalamus back to the cortex.

Its accomplishment is calibration: it makes movement smooth, accurate, and well-timed by learning and running internal models that predict the sensory consequences of actions and cancel the errors.

It is tagged non-cognitive because its principal and defining role is calibration of an effector system, though its newest lobes run the same calibration on cognitive streams, a fact this chapter records rather than centres.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The cerebral cortex, motor and association areas alike, is the source of the copies of commands and plans that the cerebellum learns to predict.

• The pontine nuclei, in the front of the brainstem, relay cortical signals into the cerebellum as mossy fibres.

• The cerebellar cortex, a three-layered sheet of granule cells, Purkinje cells, and interneurons, is the learning machine where predictions are formed.

• The inferior olive, a nucleus in the medulla, is the source of the climbing fibres that carry the error signal.

• The deep cerebellar nuclei, chiefly the dentate for the newest lobes, are the sole output and hold the running prediction.

• The motor and ventrolateral thalamus returns the correction to the cortex, closing the loop.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

Two input interfaces and one output interface define the system.

• The mossy-fibre interface, from the pons and spinal cord, delivers the context and the efference copy (a duplicate of the outgoing command); its cargo is the state of the body and the plan, and it fans out enormously onto granule cells.

• The climbing-fibre interface, from the inferior olive, delivers the teaching signal; its cargo is error, one powerful fibre wrapping each Purkinje cell and firing when the outcome differed from the prediction.

• The output interface, from the deep nuclei through the thalamus to cortex and down to brainstem motor centres, delivers the correction and the forward prediction; its format is a continuous adjustment applied to the ongoing movement.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

The incoming mossy-fibre signal is a mixture of the current bodily state and a copy of the command about to be executed.

The granule-cell layer expands this into an enormous, sparse code, a very high-dimensional representation in which many combinations of context become separately addressable.

The Purkinje cells read this expanded code and compute a prediction: what the sensory consequences of the movement should be.

The climbing fibre delivers the mismatch between prediction and reality, and where they disagree it depresses the responsible Purkinje synapses, nudging the prediction toward truth.

The deep nuclei output is therefore a forward model: a fast internal simulation of the body that runs slightly ahead of the real limb, so the system can correct a movement before the slow sensory feedback has even arrived.

The central content is that the cerebellum turns raw commands into calibrated, anticipated ones by continuously learning the body's own physics.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

It runs the expansion-recoding motif in the granule layer, mapping a modest input into a vast sparse code for fine discrimination.

It runs the supervised-learning motif at the Purkinje synapse, where a dedicated error wire, the climbing fibre, teaches by depressing active synapses that led to mistakes.

It runs the feedforward-inhibition and timing motifs among the molecular-layer interneurons, which shape the exact millisecond of the correction.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

The cerebellum is the brain's timing specialist, operating on the scale of tens to hundreds of milliseconds.

Its forward model runs ahead of real sensory feedback, which lags by around 100 milliseconds, so the cerebellum's predictive output is what makes fast movement possible at all.

Climbing-fibre teaching events are rare, about one per second per Purkinje cell, but each is decisive.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The human cerebellum holds on the order of 60 to 80 billion granule cells, roughly four times the number of neurons in the whole cerebral cortex, making it the most numerous cell type in the brain.

Its Purkinje cells number in the tens of millions, each receiving up to about 200,000 parallel-fibre inputs but only a single climbing fibre.

The limit is that its learning is slow and incremental and its output is corrective, not generative: it cannot start a movement, only refine one, so cerebellar damage leaves strength intact but accuracy destroyed.

MODULATION AND GATING:

The system is modulated by noradrenaline and serotonin from the brainstem, which adjust the gain and the readiness to learn.

The inferior olive gates learning by controlling when climbing-fibre error signals are sent, and its cells are electrically coupled so that errors are broadcast in synchronised bursts.

The chief gate on plasticity is therefore the olive's decision to signal an error at all.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

The signature plasticity is long-term depression at the parallel-fibre-to-Purkinje synapse, triggered when a climbing-fibre error coincides with parallel-fibre activity, first proposed by Marr, Albus, and Ito.

Complementary potentiation and plasticity in the deep nuclei consolidate what the cortex has learned.

This is supervised learning in the strict sense: an external teacher (error) shapes the response, unlike the reward-based learning of the basal ganglia.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The cerebellum develops late and fast; its granule cells are generated after birth from an external germinal layer, and the human cerebellum triples in volume in the first year.

This late, prolonged development makes it especially vulnerable to premature birth, and disruptions here are increasingly linked to autism and to motor and cognitive delay.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The cerebellum is an ancient hindbrain structure present across vertebrates, but its lateral lobes and the dentate nucleus expanded dramatically in the primate and especially the human line, in step with the prefrontal cortex they connect to.

That co-expansion is the anatomical basis for the modern view that the cerebellum calibrates thought as it calibrates movement.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

Thyroid hormone is essential for cerebellar development, and its deficiency in early life causes lasting cerebellar and motor impairment.

Sex and stress hormones modulate cerebellar function modestly, but the structure is not part of an endocrine axis; its coupling is developmental and permissive.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake, the cerebellum continuously predicts and corrects movement.

In slow-wave sleep, it participates in offline consolidation of newly learned motor skills.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep, cerebellar and inferior-olive activity persist and may contribute to the refinement of motor memories.

Under stress, cerebellar timing can degrade, showing as clumsiness and tremor.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Cerebellar damage causes ataxia: movements overshoot and undershoot their targets (dysmetria), lose smoothness, and lose timing.

It causes intention tremor, a shake that worsens as the hand approaches a target, and scanning, poorly timed speech.

Damage to the newest lobes causes the cerebellar cognitive-affective syndrome, with impaired planning and blunted or disinhibited emotion, the cognitive echo of dysmetria.

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

The cerebellum makes movement look effortless and thought feel fluent.

With it, actions are smooth, timed, and anticipated; without it, the same muscles and the same commands produce a jerky, overshooting, poorly timed caricature of the intended act.

It colours the organism with grace and, in its cognitive lobes, with the smoothness of well-rehearsed thinking.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Efference copies of commands and plans from cortex via the pons (mossy fibres); bodily state from the spinal cord; error signals from the inferior olive (climbing fibres, about one per second per cell).

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ A forward-model correction and prediction via the deep nuclei and thalamus to cortex and to brainstem motor centres, applied continuously to the ongoing movement.

• STATE.

◦ Purkinje-cell prediction, granule-layer sparse code, per-synapse weights set by past errors, deep-nucleus running output.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ 60 to 80 billion granule cells; tens of millions of Purkinje cells; up to about 200,000 parallel-fibre inputs per Purkinje cell but exactly one climbing fibre; feedback lag being corrected around 100 milliseconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Prediction and correction tens to hundreds of milliseconds; climbing-fibre teaching events about once per second; skill learning over trials, hours, and days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Ataxia and dysmetria (overshoot and undershoot); intention tremor; cerebellar cognitive-affective syndrome from newest-lobe damage.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline this system is the forward_model_calibration_engine.

It subscribes to a topic of efference copies published by every motor and planning module and to an error topic published by the inferior olive, and it publishes a predicted-consequence-and-correction signal back to the subscribing module.

Its nearest engineering analogue is a supervised online learner wrapped around a plant as a forward model in a control loop: it predicts the plant's next state, compares it with the measured state, and back-propagates the error to reduce it.

The biological difference is that the teaching error arrives on a single dedicated wire per output cell rather than as a dense gradient, that learning is expressed mainly as synaptic depression rather than symmetric weight update, and that one uniform microcircuit is stamped out billions of times to calibrate motor and cognitive streams alike.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A crystalline sheet holding four-fifths of the brain's neurons, taught by a single error wire per output cell, that never starts a movement but predicts and corrects every one, so that its loss spares strength and destroys accuracy.


CHAPTER 11. THE OCULOMOTOR SYSTEM (Formal descriptor: gaze-control saccadic and pursuit pathway) (Plain-English Coined Name: The Fast-Aim Gaze Rig) (NON-COG)

The eyes are the fastest-moving parts of the body, flicking from word to word up to three or four times a second, each flick a ballistic leap too fast to steer once launched.

A dedicated machine plans where to look, decides when to release the leap, and fires a precisely metered burst to the eye muscles, all while a standing brake holds the eyes still between leaps.

It is a motor system stripped to its essentials, which is exactly why it is the best-understood movement circuit in the brain.

Fast-Aim names its two jobs: aiming gaze at a target, very fast.

Gaze names its object: the direction of the eyes, not the limbs.

Rig names its specialised, purpose-built character: a small set of nuclei doing one thing supremely well.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The oculomotor system is the network that controls where the eyes point, generating rapid target-acquiring jumps (saccades), smooth tracking of moving targets (pursuit), and gaze-holding between movements.

Its accomplishment is to place the high-resolution centre of the retina on whatever matters, fast enough that vision is not disrupted, and to hold it there stably.

It is tagged non-cognitive because it is an effector system that executes gaze commands, even though the targets it aims at are chosen by cognitive attention and value systems.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The frontal eye fields, in the frontal cortex, issue voluntary saccade commands and select where to look next.

• The parietal eye fields and the lateral intraparietal area convert attention and salience into candidate gaze targets.

• The superior colliculus, a layered midbrain structure, holds a map of gaze targets and issues the command to move, competing candidates settling into a single motor burst.

• The paramedian pontine reticular formation and the rostral midbrain reticular formation are the brainstem burst generators that produce the actual velocity command, horizontal in the pons and vertical in the midbrain.

• The omnipause neurons, in the nucleus raphe interpositus, are the standing brake that must be switched off for any saccade to occur.

• The oculomotor, trochlear, and abducens nuclei are the motoneuron pools that drive the six muscles of each eye.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The system's interfaces are unusually crisp.

• The cortex-to-colliculus interface delivers a chosen target location as a point on a retinotopic map; the frontal eye fields both excite the colliculus and, via the basal ganglia oculomotor loop, release its brake.

• The colliculus-to-burst-generator interface delivers a go command; cargo is the desired change in eye position, to be converted into a velocity command.

• The omnipause interface is a gate: the colliculus and frontal eye fields inhibit the omnipause neurons, which stop clamping the burst neurons and let the saccade fire.

• The burst-generator-to-motoneuron interface delivers a pulse of velocity plus a step of position, the pulse to move the eye fast and the step to hold it in the new place against elastic recoil.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the cortex and colliculus the representation is spatial: where to look, coded as a location on a map.

The transformation at the burst generator is a change of code from place to rate: the map location is converted into a temporal burst of spikes whose duration and frequency set the size and speed of the saccade (the more spikes, the bigger the jump).

A neural integrator then converts the velocity pulse into the position step needed to hold the eye still afterwards, mathematically integrating the burst.

The central content is a coordinate-and-code transformation: a desired place becomes a metered burst becomes a held new position, all in under a tenth of a second.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

It runs the winner-take-all target-selection motif in the colliculus, where candidate targets compete on a map.

It runs the disinhibitory gate motif at the omnipause neurons, a standing brake released for the duration of the movement, driven through the basal ganglia oculomotor loop of chapter 9.

It runs the pulse-step generator and neural-integrator motif in the brainstem, converting a burst into a maintained position.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Saccades are the fastest movements the body makes, reaching angular velocities up to about 500 to 600 degrees per second and lasting less than 100 milliseconds.

Between saccades the eyes fixate for a couple of hundred milliseconds, and the whole look-plan-fire cycle repeats three to four times a second during reading or scanning.

The command is ballistic: once the burst fires, the saccade cannot be redirected mid-flight.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

Humans make on the order of three to four saccades per second, roughly 150,000 to 200,000 a day.

Saccade latency, the delay from target appearance to movement onset, is about 200 milliseconds for ordinary saccades and can drop to around 100 milliseconds for reflexive express saccades.

The system's limit is ballistic accuracy: because the leap cannot be corrected in flight, its precision depends entirely on how well the target was located and the burst metered beforehand, and any drift must be fixed by a small corrective saccade afterward.

MODULATION AND GATING:

The system is gated by the basal ganglia oculomotor loop, whose output nucleus tonically inhibits the colliculus and lifts that inhibition to permit a saccade, exactly mirroring the motor loop of chapter 9.

Dopamine, acetylcholine, and attention-related noradrenaline modulate the readiness and threshold of saccade initiation.

The frontline gate is the omnipause neuron, the last brake before the eye moves.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

The system is continuously recalibrated by the cerebellum, which keeps saccades accurate by adjusting the burst whenever saccades systematically overshoot or undershoot (saccadic adaptation).

This is why a person whose eye muscles weaken can slowly relearn accurate gaze, and why cerebellar damage makes saccades chronically dysmetric.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Saccades are present in rudimentary form at birth and mature over the first months as fixation and smooth pursuit come online.

Voluntary control from the frontal eye fields, including the ability to suppress a reflexive look toward a sudden target (the antisaccade), matures slowly through childhood and adolescence, tracking prefrontal development.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The brainstem gaze machinery is ancient and shared across vertebrates, since all animals with mobile eyes need to aim them.

The superior colliculus is the homologue of the optic tectum that dominates the fish and amphibian brain; in mammals the cortical eye fields were layered on top, adding voluntary and cognitive control to an ancient reflexive aimer.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

The oculomotor system has no significant endocrine coupling of its own; its performance depends on general metabolic and arousal states rather than on any specific hormone, so this aspect is minimal for this system.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake, the system makes purposeful, target-driven saccades and pursuit.

In slow-wave sleep, the eyes are largely still.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep, the same burst machinery fires the rapid eye movements that name the state, driven internally by the brainstem rather than by external targets.

Under fatigue and stress, saccades slow and latencies lengthen, a measurable sign of drowsiness used in vigilance testing.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Damage to the burst generators or their pathways causes gaze palsies, an inability to move the eyes in a given direction.

Progressive supranuclear palsy, a Parkinson-like disorder, characteristically abolishes vertical saccades first.

Cerebellar disease causes dysmetric, overshooting saccades, and internuclear ophthalmoplegia from a brainstem lesion decouples the two eyes so they no longer move together.

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system gives the organism an active, exploring gaze: the darting, purposeful sampling of the world that underlies reading, face-reading, and visual search.

Its rhythm is so characteristic that a person's pattern of looking reveals what they attend to and, in disease, what they cannot move toward.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Chosen gaze targets from frontal and parietal eye fields; salience maps from attention systems; a release signal from the basal ganglia oculomotor loop; recalibration from the cerebellum.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ A metered velocity pulse and position step to the eye-muscle motoneuron pools; corollary signals reporting the intended eye movement to the visual system.

• STATE.

◦ Collicular target-map activity, omnipause brake on or off, burst-neuron firing, neural-integrator position estimate.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Peak saccade velocity up to 500 to 600 degrees per second; saccade duration under 100 milliseconds; latency about 200 milliseconds, express saccades near 100; three to four saccades per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Saccade duration tens of milliseconds; fixation about 200 milliseconds; saccadic adaptation over tens to hundreds of trials.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Gaze palsy from burst-generator lesions; vertical-saccade loss in progressive supranuclear palsy; dysmetric saccades in cerebellar disease; internuclear ophthalmoplegia from brainstem lesions.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The oculomotor system maps to a engineering module named gaze_targeting_pipeline.

It subscribes to salience and target coordinates and publishes rapid pointing commands for the system's highest-bandwidth sensor.

The nearest analogue is an attention-driven sensor-pointing controller - the biological difference is the saccadic all-or-none ballistic policy with suppression of input during movement.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The fastest motor system in the body, firing ballistic leaps up to 600 degrees per second that cannot be steered once launched, held still between leaps by a standing brake of omnipause neurons that must be switched off before an eye can move.


CHAPTER 12. THE AUTONOMIC MOTOR SYSTEM (Formal descriptor: visceromotor sympathetic and parasympathetic outflow with central autonomic network) (Plain-English Coined Name: The Housekeeping Outflow) (NON-COG)

There is a motor system you never command and rarely notice, running the heart, the gut, the glands, the pupils, and the vessels every second of every day.

It has two opposing hands: one that readies the body to fight or flee, and one that quiets it to rest and digest, and health is the moment-to-moment balance struck between them.

Its commands never reach a skeletal muscle; they reach the organs, and they are set not by will but by a network in the brainstem and forebrain reading the body's inner state.

Housekeeping names its content: the involuntary regulation of the internal organs.

Outflow names its direction: it is an efferent, effector system, commands leaving for the viscera.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The autonomic motor system is the involuntary visceromotor outflow that controls smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, and glands, organised into a sympathetic division (readying the body for exertion) and a parasympathetic division (restoring and conserving), coordinated by a central autonomic network in the brainstem, hypothalamus, and limbic forebrain.

Its accomplishment is homeostasis in action: it holds blood pressure, heart rate, body temperature, digestion, and glandular secretion within safe ranges and shifts them rapidly to meet demand.

It is tagged non-cognitive because it is a regulatory effector system operating below awareness and deliberation.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The insular cortex and anterior cingulate cortex are the visceral sensory and control cortex, representing the body's internal state and biasing autonomic tone.

• The amygdala and hypothalamus set autonomic responses to emotion and to homeostatic need, the hypothalamus being the master integrator.

• The periaqueductal grey in the midbrain organises coordinated autonomic-and-behavioural response patterns such as freezing or defence.

• The nucleus tractus solitarius in the medulla is the main visceral sensory relay, receiving baroreceptor, chemoreceptor, and gut afferents.

• The rostral ventrolateral medulla is the master sympathetic command centre, setting resting blood pressure by driving spinal sympathetic neurons.

• The nucleus ambiguus and dorsal vagal nucleus are the parasympathetic command centres for the heart and viscera.

• The sympathetic preganglionic neurons of the spinal cord and the parasympathetic outflow of the vagus and sacral nerves are the final motor stations to the organs.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The system's interfaces run on a two-neuron chain from the central nervous system to each organ.

• The afferent interface: baroreceptors, chemoreceptors, and gut and organ sensors report inner state to the nucleus tractus solitarius; cargo is blood pressure, blood chemistry, and organ status.

• The central interface: the nucleus tractus solitarius routes this, via the caudal ventrolateral medulla, to the rostral ventrolateral medulla and to the vagal nuclei, which set sympathetic and parasympathetic tone.

• The sympathetic efferent interface: preganglionic neurons in the thoracolumbar cord synapse in ganglia, and postganglionic fibres release noradrenaline onto organs.

• The parasympathetic efferent interface: the vagus and sacral outflow synapse in ganglia near the organs and release acetylcholine, the adrenal medulla being a special case that releases adrenaline straight into the blood.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

The incoming representation is interoceptive: raw measurements of the body's internal variables.

The transformation in the central autonomic network is from measurement to setpoint error and then to a two-handed motor command: how much to raise sympathetic tone and how much to lower parasympathetic tone, or the reverse.

The baroreflex is the clearest example: a fall in blood pressure quiets the baroreceptors, which disinhibits the rostral ventrolateral medulla, which raises sympathetic outflow and speeds the heart while the vagus is withdrawn, restoring pressure within a heartbeat or two.

The central content is a continuous negative-feedback regulation, translating deviations of the inner milieu into balanced adjustments of two opposing effector hands.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

It runs the negative-feedback regulatory-loop motif throughout, comparing a sensed variable against a setpoint and correcting the difference.

It runs the mutual-antagonism motif between the two divisions, where raising one hand typically lowers the other for a faster net change.

It runs the two-neuron relay-and-ganglion motif on the efferent side, giving the periphery a local integrating station.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

The system spans a wide range of speeds.

The parasympathetic influence on the heart is fast and beat-to-beat, acting within a fraction of a second through the vagus.

The sympathetic influence is slower to build, over seconds, and the hormonal adrenal arm, once triggered, lasts minutes because it works through the bloodstream.

The baroreflex corrects a pressure change within one or two heartbeats.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The system controls essentially every organ continuously and never rests.

Its limit is that it is comparatively slow and coarse compared with skeletal motor control, and its two divisions cannot both be maximally active in a coordinated way, so extreme co-activation, as in some faints, causes a dangerous crash of heart rate and pressure.

Resting balance normally favours parasympathetic tone at the heart, which is why a healthy resting heart rate sits well below the heart's intrinsic pacemaker rate.

MODULATION AND GATING:

The system is modulated from above by emotion via the amygdala, by homeostatic need via the hypothalamus, and by the body's own hormones, and from below by circulating adrenaline that amplifies sympathetic effects.

Its gates sit at the medullary command centres, chiefly the rostral ventrolateral medulla for the sympathetic hand and the vagal nuclei for the parasympathetic hand.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

Autonomic responses can be conditioned: a neutral cue paired with threat comes to trigger a full sympathetic response, which is the basis of the fear-conditioned racing heart and of the measurable skin-conductance response used in lie-detection.

Baroreflex sensitivity is trainable and rises with aerobic fitness, and it falls with age and in heart disease, a change that predicts risk.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The autonomic neurons derive from the neural crest, the migratory embryonic cells that also form much of the peripheral nervous system, and the two divisions and their ganglia are laid down before birth.

Autonomic control matures over infancy, and its immaturity contributes to the fragile temperature and heart-rate regulation of newborns and prematures.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The autonomic outflow is an ancient vertebrate system, older than the skeletal motor cortex, because regulating the inner organs long predates the elaborate control of limbs.

The fight-or-flight and rest-and-digest opposition is conserved across vertebrates, and the vagus in particular carries a deep evolutionary story of increasing influence over the mammalian heart.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

This is the system's richest interface, because the autonomic and endocrine systems are two arms of one homeostatic controller.

The sympathetic system drives the adrenal medulla to release adrenaline and noradrenaline into the blood, and it triggers the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal stress response that ends in cortisol.

It regulates insulin and glucagon release from the pancreas, controls renin release and thus blood pressure and salt balance, and its parasympathetic arm promotes the hormones of digestion; conversely thyroid hormone raises the responsiveness of the whole sympathetic system, which is why an overactive thyroid mimics chronic sympathetic overdrive.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake and active, the balance shifts toward sympathetic tone.

In slow-wave sleep, parasympathetic tone dominates, heart rate and blood pressure fall, and the body restores itself.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep, autonomic control becomes irregular and surges of both divisions occur, which is one reason heart attacks cluster in the early morning during dream-rich sleep.

Under acute stress the fight-or-flight response mobilises the whole sympathetic and adrenal apparatus at once.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Autonomic failure causes orthostatic hypotension, a fall of blood pressure on standing that brings faintness, seen in pure autonomic failure and in multiple system atrophy.

Diabetes damages autonomic nerves (autonomic neuropathy), impairing heart-rate control, digestion, and bladder function.

Chronic sympathetic overdrive contributes to high blood pressure and heart failure, and disorders of the fight-or-flight response underlie panic attacks and some faints (vasovagal syncope).

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system is the body's mood in the physiological sense: the racing heart of fear, the flush of anger, the calm heaviness of a full meal, the cold sweat of dread.

It colours the organism with the felt bodily tone that emotion theory identifies as a large part of feeling itself, and it does so continuously, whether or not we notice.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Interoceptive signals (blood pressure via baroreceptors, blood chemistry via chemoreceptors, gut and organ status); emotional drive from the amygdala; homeostatic demand from the hypothalamus; circulating hormones including adrenaline, cortisol, and thyroid hormone.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Sympathetic noradrenergic outflow to organs and adrenaline into the blood; parasympathetic cholinergic outflow via the vagus and sacral nerves; heart rate, blood pressure, glandular, and smooth-muscle commands.

• STATE.

◦ Sympathetic tone, parasympathetic (vagal) tone, baroreflex setpoint, adrenal activation level.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Two divisions in mutual antagonism; two-neuron efferent chains; baroreflex correction within one to two heartbeats; resting balance favouring vagal tone at the heart.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Vagal heart effect a fraction of a second; sympathetic build-up over seconds; adrenal hormonal effect over minutes; baroreflex within one to two heartbeats.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Orthostatic hypotension in pure autonomic failure and multiple system atrophy; autonomic neuropathy in diabetes; hypertension and heart failure from chronic sympathetic overdrive; vasovagal syncope and panic from response dysregulation.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

The autonomic motor system maps to a engineering module named visceral_effector_system.

It subscribes to the central autonomic network's state decisions and publishes paired opponent commands on the sympathetic and parasympathetic channels.

The nearest analogue is a plant-management control bus with two opposing setpoint drivers - the biological difference is that the two channels are co-active patterns, not a single dial.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A two-handed involuntary outflow that never reaches a skeletal muscle, one hand readying the body for exertion and the other quieting it for repair, balanced beat-to-beat by a medullary network and married so tightly to the hormones that it and the endocrine system are two arms of one controller.


PART THREE. THE MEMORY AND COGNITIVE SYSTEMS

The systems of this part are the ones the reader is most tempted to call the mind itself.

Each is a chain of Layer 9 regions that stores, prices, words, governs, or models experience, and each is separable from the others by lesion, by dementia, and by development.

A patient can lose remembered episodes and keep meanings, lose meanings and keep habits, lose habits and keep the blackboard of the present moment.

That dissociability is the deepest fact of this part: cognition is not one machine but a federation of machines, and the following eight chapters catalogue the federation one member at a time.

All eight are tagged (COG), because each performs a signature computation that can be written down and run.


CHAPTER 13. THE EPISODIC MEMORY SYSTEM (Hippocampal-Diencephalic Circuit) (The Experience-Recorder Loop) (COG)

Every event of your life that you can still replay - the room, the faces, the order in which things happened - exists because a loop of stations first sketched it in minutes and then spent years copying it outward into the cortex.

This is the system of James Papez's 1937 circuit, rediscovered by every amnesic patient since: hippocampus to fornix to mammillary bodies to anterior thalamus to cingulate and back.

Damage any link and the past still exists, but the future stops being recorded.

Experience names the cargo: not facts but happenings, each stamped with place and time.

Recorder names the job: a fast, one-shot writing device for events that occur only once.

Loop names the anatomy: the pathway is famously a closed ring, and its consolidation dialogue with cortex is a second, slower loop.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The episodic memory system binds the scattered cortical representation of one event - what, where, when, who - into a single retrievable index, stores that index rapidly in the hippocampus, and then, over weeks to years, teaches the cortex to hold the event without hippocampal help.

Its accomplishment is one-shot learning: a single occurrence, never repeated, can be replayed decades later.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The perirhinal and parahippocampal cortices, the medial temporal item and scene gateways, which funnel object identity and spatial context respectively toward the hippocampus.

• The entorhinal cortex, the grid-mapped portal through which essentially all cortical traffic enters and leaves the hippocampus.

• The hippocampal formation, sections on the dentate gyrus, CA3 and CA1, which separates similar patterns, stores the index by one-shot synaptic change, and completes partial cues back into whole events.

• The subiculum, the hippocampal output stage, launching the fornix.

• The mammillary bodies of the hypothalamus, the diencephalic relay whose shrinkage tracks recall performance across patient groups.

• The anterior thalamic nuclei, the thalamic hub whose destruction alone is sufficient for diencephalic amnesia.

• The retrosplenial and posterior cingulate cortex, the scene-and-orientation station returning the loop toward cortex.

• The medial prefrontal cortex, the schema station that hosts consolidated, cortex-resident versions of old memories and applies retrieval strategy.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The perforant path carries entorhinal cortex output to the dentate gyrus and CA fields, format sparse cortical feature codes.

The fornix, roughly 1.2 million axons per side, carries subicular output to the mammillary bodies and septum, format event-index spike sequences.

The mammillothalamic tract carries mammillary output to the anterior thalamus, a compact bundle whose interruption reproduces Korsakoff-type amnesia.

The cingulum bundle returns anterior thalamic and cingulate traffic to retrosplenial, parahippocampal and prefrontal cortex, closing Papez's ring.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

In perirhinal and parahippocampal cortex the representation is still modality-sorted: an object stream and a scene stream, not yet one event.

The entorhinal cortex reformats it onto grid and object-vector codes, a coordinate system for experience.

The dentate gyrus performs pattern separation, mapping similar inputs onto nearly non-overlapping cell sets, with only about 2 to 4 percent of its roughly 15 million granule cells per side active in any environment.

CA3, with its recurrent collaterals, stores the conjunction as an attractor, so that any fragment - a smell, a word - can later reinstate the whole, which is pattern completion.

CA1 compares the completed memory with current entorhinal reality and exports the difference, and by the diencephalic stations the representation has become a compact index plus a temporal-order signal, which the cingulate and prefrontal stations translate back into cortical reinstatement.

Each hand-off therefore adds one thing: separation, then conjunction, then completion, then comparison, then scheduling of the slow copy to cortex.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):
