◦ A global arousal and gain signal to the entire forebrain, read by alpha-2, alpha-1, and beta adrenoceptors, plus a phasic network-reset pulse.

• STATE.

◦ Tonic firing rate, phasic burst amplitude, ambient noradrenaline level, adrenoceptor occupancy, effective target gain.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Gain factor on target neurons; exploration-exploitation balance set by tonic level; inverted-U optimum; roughly 45,000 to 50,000 neurons per side.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Phasic burst a few hundred milliseconds; gain change seconds; arousal and exploration drift minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Chronic overactivity produces anxiety and post-traumatic stress disorder; early degeneration contributes to Alzheimer and Parkinson disease; underactivity contributes to attention-deficit hyperactivity disorder.


THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline the system is named arousal_gain_broadcast.

It subscribes to salience, threat, and unexpected_change topics and publishes a global gain_level parameter plus a phasic network_reset event that every subscribing pipeline honours.

Its nearest engineering analogue is a global gain or temperature parameter combined with an interrupt line that flushes the current state of a set of processes.

The biological difference is that the gain is delivered as a diffuse chemical whose effect is non-monotonic, following an inverted U rather than rising cleanly with dose.


ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to apply noradrenergic gain to a target neuron's response and to issue a network reset when a large phasic burst arrives.

Create a number variable called tonic level with initial value 0.3.

Create a number variable called phasic burst with initial value 0.

Create a number variable called reset threshold with initial value 0.7.

Set the gain to a value that rises then falls with tonic level, so that it is highest at a middle level and lower at the extremes.

Calculate the product of the target input and the gain, and call it the sharpened response.

Check if the phasic burst is greater than the reset threshold.

If that is true, then perform the following actions.

    Announce a network reset so that target networks abandon their current state.

End the conditional block.

Display the sharpened response and whether a reset occurred.

The procedure is complete.


CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because both ideas, the inverted-U gain curve and the reset threshold, fit in a few clear lines that print the whole behaviour across arousal levels.

# Noradrenergic gain modulation and network reset from the locus coeruleus.
# Gain follows an inverted-U in tonic arousal; a large phasic burst resets
# target networks (Aston-Jones & Cohen 2005; Bouret & Sara 2005).
import math

def gain(tonic):
    # Inverted-U: peaks at a moderate tonic level, falls at the extremes.
    return math.exp(-((tonic - 0.5) ** 2) / (2 * 0.15 ** 2))

def lc_step(target_input, tonic, phasic, reset_threshold=0.7):
    sharpened = target_input * gain(tonic)
    reset = phasic > reset_threshold
    return sharpened, reset

signal = 1.0
for tonic in (0.1, 0.5, 0.9):            # drowsy, optimal, over-aroused
    resp, _ = lc_step(signal, tonic, phasic=0.0)
    print(f"tonic={tonic}: gain-sharpened response = {resp:.2f}")

resp, reset = lc_step(signal, tonic=0.5, phasic=0.9)   # salient event
print("large phasic burst -> network reset:", reset)


WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A grain-sized blue nucleus of a few tens of thousands of cells that touches the entire forebrain, whose tonic level sets a knife-edge inverted-U of attention and whose phasic burst can wipe the whole cortex's slate clean for a fresh start.


CHAPTER 33. THE SEROTONIN SYSTEM (The Raphe Serotonergic Projection) (The Patience-and-Mood Broadcast) (COG)

Along the midline seam of the brainstem sit the cells that decide whether waiting is worth it, and colour the emotional wash over everything you do, speaking a chemical language that the rest of the brain hears through more different receptors than any other transmitter.

This is the most receptor-diverse broadcast in the brain and the slowest to change your outlook.

Patience names one of its clearest jobs: it supports the willingness to wait for a delayed reward rather than grab a smaller one now.

Mood names its most famous office: it sets the slow background tone of feeling that antidepressant medicines target.

Broadcast names its reach: from the midline raphe nuclei to nearly the whole forebrain, read through at least fourteen receptor subtypes.


WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The serotonin system is the set of midline brainstem neurons that make serotonin, a signalling molecule of the indoleamine family also called 5-hydroxytryptamine, and release it across the forebrain to set patience, mood, and the readiness of circuits to change.

Serotonin is abbreviated in the literature as 5-HT, standing for 5-hydroxytryptamine, and that short form is used from here on within this chapter.

Its central accomplishment is to bias the trade-off between waiting and acting, supporting patience and the suppression of impulsive responses.

Its second accomplishment is to colour mood over long timescales and to gate plasticity, opening and closing windows in which target circuits can be rewired.


THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The dorsal raphe nucleus, the largest midline serotonin cell group of the midbrain, is the main source for the cortex, striatum, and limbic system.

• The median raphe nucleus, a neighbouring midline group, supplies the hippocampus and septum and shapes theta rhythm.

• The prefrontal cortex, the frontal association sheet, is a target where 5-HT tunes impulse control and flexible choice.

• The striatum, the action-selection input hub, is a target where 5-HT interacts with dopamine to set the discounting of delayed reward.

• The amygdala and hippocampus, the emotion and memory nuclei, are targets where 5-HT sets emotional tone and gates plasticity.


THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The ascending serotonergic projection runs from the raphe nuclei forward through the medial forebrain bundle to the entire forebrain; origin raphe, destination cortex, striatum, and limbic structures, cargo a patience and mood signal, format slow tonic firing with modest phasic modulation.

The interface at each target is the most elaborate receptor field in the brain: at least fourteen 5-HT receptor subtypes across seven families, all but one being slow G-protein-coupled receptors and one, the 5-HT3 receptor, being a fast ion channel.

Because different targets express different receptor mixes, the same released 5-HT can excite one region and inhibit another, which is why the system's behavioural effects are so diverse and sometimes opposite.


THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the source the representation is a slow tonic level plus a modest signal that rises while an animal successfully waits for a delayed reward, the clearest physiological correlate of patience.

Where dopamine encodes how good an outcome is, 5-HT encodes something closer to how long it is worth holding out and how to weight the far future against the near.

In the target the signal is transformed by the local receptor mix: through the inhibitory 5-HT1A receptor it quietens a region, through the excitatory 5-HT2A receptor it energises another, and through receptors such as 5-HT4 and 5-HT7 it sets the gain on plasticity itself.

The hand-off therefore does not deliver one lesson but a region-specific retuning, lengthening the horizon over which the striatum discounts reward while opening or closing plasticity windows in the cortex and hippocampus.


THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

At source it runs a slow pacemaker motif, autoinhibited by its own 5-HT1A autoreceptors so the system self-limits.

At target it runs a receptor-demultiplexing motif, one transmitter routed to opposite effects by which receptor a cell carries.

In the hippocampus it participates in the synaptic-tagging-and-capture motif, marking recently active synapses for later strengthening.


TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Raphe neurons fire slowly and very regularly at roughly 0.5 to 3 hertz, one of the steadiest rhythms in the brain, and fall silent in rapid-eye-movement sleep.

The patience signal builds over seconds of waiting.

The mood-setting effects operate over hours to weeks, which is why antidepressants raise 5-HT within hours but lift mood only after two to six weeks, the delay reflecting slow downstream plasticity rather than the transmitter level itself.


CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The human raphe complex holds on the order of a few hundred thousand serotonin neurons, again a small population for a forebrain-wide reach.

The output is low-bandwidth and slow, suited to setting a background bias rather than carrying fast content.

The system's power comes not from its firing but from its receptor diversity, the at-least-fourteen subtypes that let one transmitter act as many.


MODULATION AND GATING:

The system is gated by its own 5-HT1A autoreceptors, which throttle firing, the desensitisation of which over weeks is thought to underlie the delayed action of antidepressants.

It is driven by the habenula and the prefrontal cortex, which report aversive and contextual information.

It interacts constantly with dopamine, the two forming an opponent pair in which 5-HT tends to favour patience and behavioural inhibition where dopamine favours vigour and action.


PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

5-HT is a powerful gate on plasticity: through several receptors it raises or lowers the threshold for long-term potentiation and depression, and it can switch its own effect with age, promoting plasticity at one stage and restraining it at another.

The psychedelic 5-HT2A agonists produce a marked, days-long increase in cortical structural plasticity, one basis for their study in depression.

By facilitating synaptic tagging and capture, 5-HT helps convert transient learning into lasting memory.


DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Raphe serotonin neurons are among the earliest-born neurons of the brain, arising from the hindbrain under the transcription factors known by the short names PET1 and LMX1B.

Serotonin acts as a developmental signal well before it acts as a neurotransmitter, guiding the wiring of sensory maps, so that disturbing it early has lifelong consequences.

The system continues to shape circuit maturation through infancy.


EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Serotonin is one of the most ancient signalling molecules, used by bacteria, plants, and the simplest animals, and it governs feeding and locomotion in the worm and mood-like states across the animal kingdom.

The vertebrate elaboration is the huge fan-out of receptor subtypes, which turns one old molecule into a flexible, region-specific controller.

Its link to satiety and gut function is ancient, and most of the body's serotonin is in fact made in the gut.


THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

The system is tightly coupled to the stress axis: cortisol regulates the enzyme that makes 5-HT and the density of its receptors, and chronic stress lowers 5-HT function.

Serotonin in turn helps regulate the release of stress and reproductive hormones from the hypothalamus.

Its precursor tryptophan is a dietary amino acid, so nutritional state directly sets how much 5-HT the brain can make.


STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake, raphe neurons fire steadily and set the day's mood and patience.

In slow-wave sleep their firing slows.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep they fall essentially silent, one of the defining features of that state and a permissive condition for its vivid imagery.

Under stress the system is initially recruited and, with chronic stress, becomes depleted and dysregulated.


DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Dysfunction of 5-HT signalling is central to major depressive disorder and to anxiety disorders, the targets of the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor medicines.

Obsessive-compulsive disorder responds specifically to serotonergic treatment, linking the system to the suppression of intrusive repetitive behaviour.

Disturbed serotonergic development is implicated in some features of autism spectrum conditions.


HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system sets the emotional weather rather than the emotional event: at healthy levels the organism can wait, tolerate frustration, and hold a stable mood, while depleted it becomes impulsive, irritable, and low.

It is the difference between letting an insult pass and snapping at it, between saving for tomorrow and spending today.

The 5-HT level is, loosely, the organism's estimate of how much it can afford to be patient and calm.


SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Aversive and contextual signals from the habenula and prefrontal cortex; stress signals from cortisol; dietary tryptophan availability.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ A patience, mood, and plasticity-gating signal to the whole forebrain, read through at least fourteen 5-HT receptor subtypes with region-specific and sometimes opposite effects.

• STATE.

◦ Tonic firing rate, ambient 5-HT level, autoreceptor sensitivity, per-region receptor mix and occupancy.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Temporal-discount horizon; behavioural-inhibition bias; per-region plasticity threshold; a few hundred thousand source neurons; fourteen or more receptor subtypes.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Firing rhythm 0.5 to 3 hertz; patience signal seconds; mood and plasticity effects hours to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Hypofunction contributes to major depression, anxiety, and obsessive-compulsive disorder; developmental disturbance is implicated in autism spectrum conditions.


THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline the system is named patience_mood_broadcast.

It subscribes to aversion, context, and stress topics and publishes a discount_horizon parameter, a mood_tone parameter, and per-region plasticity_gate flags.

Its nearest engineering analogue is the discount-factor and exploration-bias setting of a reinforcement learner combined with a per-module learning-rate schedule.

The biological difference is the demultiplexing: one published value is read through more than a dozen receptor channels, so the same broadcast raises one module's activity and lowers another's.


ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to set the patience of a chooser by using the serotonin level to adjust the temporal discount, then to choose between a smaller-sooner and a larger-later reward.

Create a number variable called serotonin level with initial value 0.5.

Create a number variable called base discount with initial value 0.6.

Set the discount factor to a value that rises towards one as serotonin level rises, so that higher serotonin means the future is discounted less.

Calculate the value of the sooner reward as its size, because it arrives now.

Calculate the value of the later reward as its size multiplied by the discount factor raised to the power of its delay.

Compare the two values.

If the later value is greater than the sooner value, then choose to wait.

Otherwise, choose the sooner reward.

Display the chosen option and the discount factor used.

The procedure is complete.


CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the patience dial is a single monotone mapping from serotonin to a discount factor, and a short loop shows how raising serotonin flips the choice from grab-now to wait.

# Serotonin sets patience by tuning the temporal-discount factor.
# Higher 5-HT lengthens the horizon over which delayed reward is valued
# (Miyazaki et al. 2011; Schweighofer et al. 2008).
def discount_factor(serotonin, base=0.6):
    # Maps 5-HT in [0,1] to a discount factor in [base, ~1].
    return base + (1.0 - base) * serotonin

def choose(sooner, later, delay, serotonin):
    gamma = discount_factor(serotonin)
    v_now = sooner                      # arrives immediately
    v_later = later * (gamma ** delay)  # discounted by the delay
    return ("wait" if v_later > v_now else "take now"), gamma

# Smaller-sooner 1.0 now versus larger-later 2.0 in 4 steps.
for serotonin in (0.1, 0.5, 0.9):       # impulsive -> patient
    choice, gamma = choose(1.0, 2.0, delay=4, serotonin=serotonin)
    print(f"5-HT={serotonin}: gamma={gamma:.2f} -> {choice}")


WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A steady, self-limiting broadcast from the brainstem seam that sets patience and mood over seconds to weeks, and is read through more receptor subtypes than any other transmitter, so one molecule can calm one region and rouse another at the same instant.


CHAPTER 34. THE ACETYLCHOLINE SYSTEM (The Basal Forebrain and Brainstem Cholinergic Projection) (The Attention-and-Encoding Broadcast) (COG)

Two hidden clusters, one under the front of the brain and one in the upper brainstem, release the molecule that first ran muscles and, in the brain, decides what is worth noticing and when to write it down.

This is the attention switch and the record button of the cortex.

Attention names its cortical office: it raises the signal-to-noise ratio of the inputs the brain has decided to track, sharpening perception of the relevant.

Encoding names its mnemonic office: it tips the cortex and hippocampus into a mode that favours storing new information over recalling old.

Broadcast names its reach: from the basal forebrain to the whole cortex and hippocampus, and from the brainstem to the thalamus, setting the state of the sensory gates.


WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The acetylcholine system is the set of basal forebrain and brainstem neurons that make acetylcholine, the signalling molecule that also drives muscle at the body's nerve-muscle junctions, and release it across the cortex, hippocampus, and thalamus to set attention and the encoding of memory.

Acetylcholine is the oldest-known neurotransmitter and here serves as the cortex's attention and learning-state signal rather than as a muscle command.

Its central accomplishment is to raise the signal-to-noise ratio of attended inputs, enhancing thalamic and cortical responses to relevant stimuli while suppressing internally generated noise.

Its second accomplishment is to bias the cortex and hippocampus towards encoding new experience rather than retrieving stored memory, setting which of those two operations dominates at a given moment.


THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The nucleus basalis of Meynert, the large cholinergic cell group of the basal forebrain, is the main source of acetylcholine for the whole neocortex.

• The medial septum and diagonal band, the medial basal forebrain groups, supply the hippocampus and set its theta rhythm.

• The pedunculopontine and laterodorsal tegmental nuclei, the brainstem cholinergic groups, supply the thalamus and set arousal and rapid-eye-movement sleep.

• The neocortex, the target sheet, has its attentional gain and cortical state set by the broadcast.

• The hippocampus, the memory-encoding structure, has its encoding-versus-retrieval balance set by acetylcholine level.


THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The cortical cholinergic projection runs from the nucleus basalis to the entire neocortex; origin nucleus basalis, destination cortex, cargo an attention and cortical-state signal, format both slow tonic release and fast, spatially targeted phasic release.

The septohippocampal pathway runs from the medial septum to the hippocampus; cargo the encoding-mode signal and the theta pacemaker, format rhythmic drive.

The brainstem projection runs from the pedunculopontine and laterodorsal tegmental nuclei to the thalamus; cargo the arousal and relay-mode signal.

The interface at each target is a two-receptor field: fast nicotinic receptors, which are ion channels, and slow muscarinic receptors, which are G-protein-coupled, letting acetylcholine act on both the millisecond and the second timescale.


THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the source the representation is a state-and-salience signal: tonic level reports the cortical state from sleep to alert wakefulness, and fast phasic transients report the arrival of behaviourally significant, attention-worthy cues, including a valence-free reinforcement signal.

In the thalamus the signal is transformed into a change of relay mode, switching thalamic cells from rhythmic burst firing, which blocks transmission, to tonic firing, which passes sensory information to cortex.

In the cortex it becomes a gain and gating change: it enhances the throughput of feedforward sensory input while suppressing the intracortical feedback that carries prior expectations, which is exactly the recipe for favouring encoding of the new over retrieval of the known.

The hand-off therefore converts one broadcast into a coordinated setting, opening the sensory gates, sharpening the attended channel, and tipping the memory system towards writing.


THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

At source it runs a state-pacemaker motif that sets the theta rhythm in the hippocampus.

At the thalamus it runs the relay-mode-switch motif, flipping cells between burst and tonic firing.

In the cortex it runs a differential-gain motif, boosting feedforward while damping feedback, the physical form of the encoding-versus-retrieval switch.


TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Tonic firing varies slowly with the state over seconds to minutes and is high in waking and rapid-eye-movement sleep and low in slow-wave sleep.

Phasic cortical transients are fast, on the order of tens to a few hundred milliseconds, and can be delivered to restricted cortical zones rather than the whole sheet.

The septohippocampal drive paces the theta rhythm at roughly 4 to 8 hertz.


CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The human nucleus basalis holds on the order of 200,000 or more cholinergic neurons, larger than the aminergic nuclei but still small against the cortex it serves.

Newer work shows the cortical broadcast is faster and more spatially specific than the old picture of a slow, uniform mist, giving it more addressing capacity than the other modulators.

Its limit is that it still sets a state and a salience flag rather than carrying content.


MODULATION AND GATING:

The system is driven by the orexin and noradrenaline systems reporting wakefulness and salience, and by the prefrontal cortex reporting task demands.

It is gated by its own presynaptic autoreceptors and by the enzyme acetylcholinesterase, which clears acetylcholine within milliseconds, the target of the drugs used to treat Alzheimer disease.

It is regulated by the reward and threat systems, which recruit phasic acetylcholine to salient events.


PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

Acetylcholine is a strong permissive signal for cortical plasticity: pairing a stimulus with nucleus basalis activity can reorganise a sensory cortical map around that stimulus, showing that the broadcast can direct where learning happens.

By favouring feedforward encoding and by lowering the threshold for potentiation, it marks attended experience as worth storing.

Its decline is one reason learning and memory falter in cholinergic disease.


DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Basal forebrain cholinergic neurons arise from the ventral telencephalon and depend for their survival on nerve growth factor supplied by their cortical targets, a target-derived dependence that makes them vulnerable when that supply falls.

Their projections mature through infancy alongside the sensory maps whose plasticity they gate.

They are among the neurons most consistently lost early in Alzheimer disease.


EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Acetylcholine is the transmitter of the neuromuscular junction across animals and one of the oldest signalling molecules pressed into neural service.

Its central use for arousal and attention is conserved across mammals.

The elaboration of a large, fast, spatially specific basal forebrain projection is a feature of the enlarged mammalian cortex.


THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

The system is modulated by cortisol and by thyroid hormone, both of which influence cholinergic function and, when abnormal, impair attention and memory.

It interacts with the reproductive hormones, and the fall in oestrogen after menopause modulates cholinergic tone, one factor in cognitive complaints of that transition.

Its metabolic dependence on the availability of choline and acetyl groups ties it to nutritional state.


STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake and attentive, acetylcholine is high and the cortex is in its encoding, externally directed mode.

In slow-wave sleep acetylcholine falls to its lowest, releasing the cortex to replay and consolidate memory, the retrieval-favouring low-acetylcholine state.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep acetylcholine rises again to waking levels while the aminergic systems stay silent, a unique chemistry thought to underlie dreaming.

Under stress the system is recruited to sharpen threat-related attention.


DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Loss of basal forebrain cholinergic neurons is a defining lesion of Alzheimer disease, and the mainstay symptomatic drugs are cholinesterase inhibitors that prolong the acetylcholine that remains.

Cholinergic dysfunction contributes to the attentional and visual-hallucination features of Lewy body dementia and Parkinson disease dementia.

Blocking muscarinic receptors, as some medicines do, produces confusion and amnesia, showing the system's necessity for clear cognition.


HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system sets whether the organism is turned outward to the world or inward to its own store: high acetylcholine and the senses are vivid and the moment is being written down, low acetylcholine and the mind drifts to memory and rehearsal.

It is the difference between studying a new face and reminiscing with eyes closed.

The acetylcholine level is the organism's estimate of how much the present is worth attending to and recording.


SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Wakefulness and salience from orexin and noradrenaline; task demands from prefrontal cortex; reward and threat signals recruiting phasic release.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ An attention and cortical-state signal to cortex, hippocampus, and thalamus, read by fast nicotinic and slow muscarinic receptors, plus a phasic salience flag.

• STATE.

◦ Tonic level, phasic transient amplitude and location, thalamic relay mode, cortical feedforward-versus-feedback balance.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Signal-to-noise gain; encoding-versus-retrieval bias; theta frequency 4 to 8 hertz; 200,000 or more source neurons; nicotinic fast and muscarinic slow channels.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Phasic transient tens to hundreds of milliseconds; clearance by acetylcholinesterase milliseconds; state drift seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Basal forebrain loss produces the amnesia of Alzheimer disease; cholinergic dysfunction contributes to Lewy body and Parkinson disease dementia; muscarinic blockade produces confusion and amnesia.


THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline the system is named attention_encoding_switch.

It subscribes to salience, task_demand, and wakefulness topics and publishes an attention_gain parameter, a per-region encoding_mode flag, and a thalamic relay_mode setting.

Its nearest engineering analogue is a read-write mode selector combined with an attentional gain and gating stage that raises the weight of the selected input channel.

The biological difference is that the same chemical acts on two receptor systems at once, one in milliseconds and one in seconds, so the switch and the gain are set by a single graded broadcast rather than by separate control lines.


ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to use the acetylcholine level to switch the cortex between an encoding mode, which favours new sensory input, and a retrieval mode, which favours stored memory.

Create a number variable called acetylcholine level with initial value 0.5.

Create a number variable called switch point with initial value 0.5.

Set the feedforward weight to rise with acetylcholine level, so that more acetylcholine passes more new sensory input.

Set the feedback weight to fall with acetylcholine level, so that more acetylcholine suppresses stored expectation.

Calculate the cortical activity as the sum of the sensory input times the feedforward weight and the memory input times the feedback weight.

Check if acetylcholine level is greater than switch point.

If that is true, then report that the cortex is in encoding mode.

Otherwise, report that the cortex is in retrieval mode.

Display the mode and the cortical activity.

The procedure is complete.


CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the encoding-versus-retrieval switch is just two opposing weights driven by one variable, and a short sweep shows the cortex hand over from writing to reading as acetylcholine falls.

# Acetylcholine sets the cortical encoding-versus-retrieval balance by
# boosting feedforward sensory drive and suppressing feedback expectation
# (Hasselmo 2006). High ACh = encode the new; low ACh = retrieve the known.
def cortical_mode(ach, sensory=1.0, memory=1.0):
    feedforward = ach            # high ACh passes new input
    feedback = 1.0 - ach         # high ACh suppresses stored expectation
    activity = sensory * feedforward + memory * feedback
    mode = "encoding" if ach > 0.5 else "retrieval"
    return mode, activity

for ach in (0.9, 0.5, 0.1):      # attentive waking -> slow-wave sleep
    mode, act = cortical_mode(ach)
    print(f"ACh={ach}: mode={mode:9s} cortical activity={act:.2f}")


WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A pair of hidden nuclei broadcasting the muscle molecule into the cortex to raise the volume on what matters and to flip the whole sheet between writing down the new and reading back the old, on two receptor timescales at once.


CHAPTER 35. THE HISTAMINE SYSTEM (The Tuberomammillary Histaminergic Projection) (The Wakefulness Broadcast) (NON-COG)

At the very floor of the brain, just above the roof of the mouth, sits the only source of histamine in the nervous system, a small nucleus that does one blunt and vital thing: it holds you awake.

This is the wake substrate, the chemical that antihistamines switch off when they make you drowsy.

Wakefulness names its single office: it supplies a tonic drive that keeps the cortex in the aroused, waking state and is absent in sleep.

Broadcast names its reach: from one nucleus in the posterior hypothalamus to the whole brain, the classic diffuse arousal design.


WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The histamine system is the single hypothalamic nucleus that makes histamine, a signalling molecule better known for its role in allergy, and releases it across the whole brain to promote and maintain wakefulness.

It is tagged (NON-COG) because it supplies the arousal substrate on which cognition runs rather than performing a computation itself.

Its central accomplishment is to sustain the waking state: its neurons fire only during wakefulness, most strongly during attentive waking, and fall silent in sleep, so that its output is close to a pure wake signal.

Removing or blocking it produces sedation, which is why the older antihistamine allergy drugs, which cross into the brain, make people sleepy.


THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The tuberomammillary nucleus, the histamine cell group in the posterior hypothalamus, is the sole brain source of histamine and the origin of the broadcast.

• The cerebral cortex, the target sheet, receives histamine that supports the waking, activated electroencephalogram.

• The thalamus, the sensory relay hub, receives histamine that keeps it in transmitting mode.

• The other arousal nuclei, including the locus coeruleus and raphe, are targets and partners that histamine helps keep active.

• The ventrolateral preoptic nucleus, the sleep-promoting group, is the mutual antagonist that histamine opposes in the sleep-wake switch.


THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The ascending histaminergic projection runs from the tuberomammillary nucleus throughout the brain; origin tuberomammillary nucleus, destination cortex, thalamus, and the other arousal nuclei, cargo a wakefulness signal, format slow tonic firing gated by the sleep-wake state.

The interface is a field of four histamine receptor subtypes: the excitatory H1 and H2 receptors that carry the postsynaptic wake-promoting action, the H3 receptor that sits on the histamine cells themselves as an autoreceptor and brakes their own release, and the H4 receptor found mainly outside the brain.

Histamine neurons also co-release the inhibitory transmitter gamma-aminobutyric acid, so the interface carries both an excitatory and a restraining message.


THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the source the representation is essentially a one-bit state signal graded by vigilance: off in sleep, low in quiet waking, moderate in active waking, and highest in attentive waking.

In the thalamus the signal is transformed, through H1 and H2 receptors, into a depolarisation that shifts relay cells towards transmitting mode.

In the cortex it becomes a general increase in excitability that supports the fast, low-amplitude electroencephalogram of the waking brain.

There is no rich informational transformation here, which is exactly why the system is tagged non-cognitive: it sets the level, not the content.


THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

At source it runs a slow autoinhibited pacemaker motif, the H3 autoreceptor limiting its own firing.

At the sleep-wake boundary it runs the mutual-inhibition flip-flop motif with the ventrolateral preoptic nucleus, two populations that inhibit each other so the system settles cleanly into either wake or sleep.


TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Histamine neurons fire slowly and irregularly at under about 10 hertz during waking and stop firing before sleep onset.

The transition between firing and silence tracks the sleep-wake state over minutes, giving the system the temporal grain of the state schedule rather than of moment-to-moment events.


CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The human brain holds on the order of 64,000 histamine neurons, one of the smallest of the broadcast nuclei.

Its output is effectively a single scalar wake level, the lowest bandwidth of any system in this part.

Its limit is definitional: it can raise or lower arousal but cannot direct attention to a particular thing.


MODULATION AND GATING:

The system is driven by the orexin system, which supplies the stabilising wake command, so histamine sits downstream of orexin in the arousal hierarchy.

It is gated by its own H3 autoreceptors and silenced by inhibitory gamma-aminobutyric acid input from the ventrolateral preoptic nucleus at sleep onset.

The H3 autoreceptor is a drug target: blocking it raises histamine release and promotes wakefulness, the mechanism of the medicine pitolisant used in narcolepsy.


PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

This section does not apply to this system in a computational sense; the histamine system supplies an arousal substrate rather than a learning signal, and any effect on memory is indirect through the general level of wakefulness.


DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Tuberomammillary histamine neurons arise in the posterior hypothalamus and are among the neurons that establish the ascending arousal system before birth.

The wake-promoting network they belong to matures over infancy as the consolidated day-night sleep pattern emerges.


EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Histamine is an ancient signalling molecule used widely in the body for immune and digestive functions, and its recruitment as a central arousal transmitter is a vertebrate specialisation.

A single small hypothalamic source broadcasting a wake signal is a conserved feature of the mammalian arousal system.


THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

The tuberomammillary nucleus sits within the hypothalamus and is influenced by the metabolic and stress signals of that region, linking arousal to energy state, and histamine helps regulate feeding and energy balance.

Its activity varies with the circadian and hormonal cycle that also governs the sleep-wake schedule.


STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake, the system fires and maintains arousal, most strongly during attentive waking.

In slow-wave sleep it is silent.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep it is also silent, unlike acetylcholine, which is part of why that state differs chemically from waking.

Under stress and heightened vigilance its firing rises with the demand for arousal.


DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Loss of orexin drive in narcolepsy leaves the histamine system less able to stabilise wakefulness, contributing to daytime sleepiness, and histamine-raising drugs treat that sleepiness.

Sedating antihistamines, by blocking H1 receptors in the brain, cause drowsiness and impair alertness.

Abnormal histamine signalling has been linked to Tourette syndrome through rare mutations in a histamine-making enzyme.


HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system does not colour experience with any particular quality; it simply supplies the wakefulness without which there is no experience at all.

Its presence is the difference between being awake and being asleep, the plainest and most fundamental setting in this part.

Turn it off and the organism drifts towards sleep no matter what else is happening.


SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Wake command from orexin; sleep-onset inhibition from the ventrolateral preoptic nucleus; metabolic and circadian signals from the hypothalamus.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ A tonic wakefulness signal to cortex, thalamus, and the other arousal nuclei, read by excitatory H1 and H2 receptors, with H3 autoreceptor self-braking.

• STATE.

◦ Firing on or off with sleep-wake state, graded by vigilance; ambient histamine level; H3 autoreceptor occupancy.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Wake-drive level; H3 self-inhibition strength; roughly 64,000 source neurons; firing under about 10 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ State transitions over minutes; firing tracks the sleep-wake schedule rather than fast events.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Insufficient stabilisation, downstream of orexin loss, contributes to the sleepiness of narcolepsy; H1 blockade causes sedation; enzyme mutations are linked to Tourette syndrome.


THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline the system is named wakefulness_substrate.

It subscribes to a wake_command topic from the orexin pipeline and a sleep_onset topic from the preoptic pipeline, and publishes a global wake_level parameter that arousal-dependent pipelines require to run.

Its nearest engineering analogue is a power-enable or clock-enable line that must be held high for downstream modules to operate.

The biological difference is that the enable is a graded chemical whose neurons also brake themselves through an autoreceptor and co-release an opposing inhibitory transmitter, so the on-state is self-limiting rather than a clean digital high.


ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).


CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).


WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The brain's only histamine source, a nucleus of some sixty-four thousand cells at the floor of the hypothalamus that fires only when awake and hardest when attentive, whose silencing by an allergy pill is felt as drowsiness.


CHAPTER 36. THE OREXIN SYSTEM (The Lateral Hypothalamic Orexinergic Projection) (The Sleep-Wake Stabiliser) (NON-COG)

A few thousand neurons in the side wall of the hypothalamus make a peptide that no other cell makes, and with it they hold the switch between sleep and waking steady, so that you stay reliably awake by day and reliably asleep by night rather than flickering between the two.

This is the finger on the sleep-wake switch, and its loss is the cause of narcolepsy.

Sleep-wake names the switch it stabilises: not the on and off themselves but the firmness with which each state is held.

Stabiliser names its office: it does not so much wake the brain as prevent unwanted transitions, keeping each state from collapsing into the other.


WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The orexin system is the small group of lateral hypothalamic neurons that make the orexin peptides, also called hypocretins, and release them onto the arousal nuclei to stabilise the boundary between sleep and wakefulness.

Orexin and hypocretin are two names for the same pair of peptides, orexin-A and orexin-B, discovered by two laboratories at once; this chapter uses orexin throughout.

It is tagged (NON-COG) because it regulates state rather than performing a computation, and it is catalogued here as a system and in the Layer 11 states volume as machinery of the sleep-wake schedule.

Its central accomplishment is to consolidate wakefulness and sleep into stable bouts, preventing the rapid, inappropriate state transitions, including sudden intrusions of dream-sleep phenomena, that appear when it is lost.


THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The lateral hypothalamic and perifornical area, the orexin cell group, is the sole source of the orexin peptides and the origin of the stabilising signal.

• The locus coeruleus, the noradrenaline nucleus, is a key target that orexin excites to sustain arousal.

• The tuberomammillary nucleus, the histamine wake source, is a target through which orexin promotes wakefulness.

• The dorsal raphe, the serotonin source, and the brainstem cholinergic nuclei are targets that orexin recruits to the waking network.

• The reward and feeding circuits, including the ventral tegmental area, are targets that couple state to motivation and energy balance.


THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The orexinergic projection runs from the lateral hypothalamus to all of the arousal nuclei and to the cortex; origin lateral hypothalamus, destination the arousal system, cargo a state-stabilising excitatory drive, format tonic firing in waking that ceases in sleep.

The interface is a field of two orexin receptor subtypes: orexin receptor 1, which prefers orexin-A and is found on the locus coeruleus and ventral tegmental area, and orexin receptor 2, which binds both peptides and is found on the tuberomammillary nucleus and raphe and is the receptor most important for sleep-wake stability.

Orexin neurons co-release the fast excitatory transmitter glutamate, so their message is both a slow peptide drive and a fast excitation.


THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the source the representation is a state-and-context signal: orexin cells fire during active waking, especially during motivated behaviour, and integrate hunger, stress, and circadian time into that drive.

Along the chain the signal is transformed into sustained excitation of every arousal nucleus at once, which is what turns a set of separately switchable nuclei into a single stable coalition.

The system therefore does not add content but adds hysteresis to the sleep-wake switch, deepening the wells of the wake state and, indirectly, of sleep, so that the brain resists tipping between them until the appropriate time.

The clearest proof of this transformation is its failure: without orexin the switch loses its stability and flickers, which is the state of narcolepsy.


THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

It runs the switch-stabilising motif, an excitatory input to both sides of a flip-flop that deepens each stable state and sharpens the transition between them.

At source it runs an integrator motif, summing metabolic, circadian, and emotional inputs into a single state-drive.


TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Orexin neurons fire tonically during waking, increase firing during active and motivated waking, and fall silent during both slow-wave and rapid-eye-movement sleep.

Orexin release follows the circadian rhythm, rising across the active day and falling towards the rest phase, giving the system a temporal grain of hours as well as of the moment-to-moment state.


CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The human hypothalamus holds on the order of 50,000 to 80,000 orexin neurons, a small population whose selective loss is nonetheless catastrophic for state stability.

Its output stabilises state rather than carrying content, so its bandwidth is that of a controller, not a channel.

Its limit is that it sets the firmness of states, not their timing, which is set by the circadian clock and the sleep-homeostatic drive it reads.


MODULATION AND GATING:

The system integrates and is gated by several inputs: it is excited by low blood glucose and by the hunger hormone ghrelin, inhibited by the satiety hormone leptin and by glucose, and driven by the circadian clock of the suprachiasmatic nucleus.

It is restrained during sleep by inhibitory input from the sleep-promoting preoptic neurons.

It is modulated by emotional and stress inputs from the limbic system, coupling arousal to motivation.


PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

This section does not apply to this system in a computational sense; the orexin system stabilises state rather than learning, though its targets and its own excitability can be shaped by chronic changes in sleep, stress, and energy balance.


DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Orexin neurons differentiate in the lateral hypothalamus during gestation and establish their broad projections to the arousal system before the mature consolidated sleep-wake pattern appears.

The consolidation of sleep into a single nightly bout over infancy tracks the maturation of this stabilising system.


EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

The orexin system is conserved across vertebrates, where it consistently links arousal, feeding, and energy expenditure, reflecting an ancient need to be awake when food must be sought.

Its dual role in state and in motivated behaviour is a conserved coupling rather than a mammalian novelty.


THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

This coupling is central to the system: orexin neurons are direct metabolic sensors, excited by ghrelin and low glucose and inhibited by leptin and high glucose, so they wire energy state to arousal, keeping a hungry animal awake to forage.

The system interacts with the stress axis, being excited by corticotropin-releasing hormone, and it influences the release of pituitary hormones, linking state to the endocrine schedule.


STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake, orexin is active and holds the waking state stable, most strongly during motivated activity.

In slow-wave sleep it is silent, permitting sleep to consolidate.

In rapid-eye-movement sleep it is also silent, and it normally prevents the intrusion of that state's muscle paralysis into waking, the failure of which produces the cataplexy of narcolepsy.

Under stress it is recruited, promoting the arousal and vigilance appropriate to threat.


DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Autoimmune destruction of the orexin neurons causes narcolepsy type 1, whose excessive daytime sleepiness, fragmented sleep, and cataplexy are exactly the loss of state stability.

Excess orexin signalling contributes to insomnia, which is why the newer sleeping medicines are orexin receptor blockers that lower the wake drive.

Orexin receptor agonists are in development to restore wakefulness in narcolepsy.


HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

This system does not colour experience with a quality but with reliability: with it, waking and sleep are solid and dependable, and without it the organism cannot trust either state to hold.

It is the difference between a night of consolidated sleep and a day of unbroken waking, and a life of dozing off mid-sentence and jerking awake all night.

Its presence is the steadiness of the state on which every colour the other systems add is painted.


SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Metabolic state from ghrelin, leptin, and glucose; circadian time from the suprachiasmatic nucleus; sleep pressure from the preoptic area; stress and emotion from the limbic system.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ A state-stabilising excitatory drive to all arousal nuclei and cortex, read by orexin receptor 1 and orexin receptor 2, with co-released glutamate.

• STATE.

◦ Firing during motivated waking, silent in sleep; ambient orexin level; integrated metabolic and circadian drive.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Wake-state stability or hysteresis; metabolic gain; circadian phase; roughly 50,000 to 80,000 source neurons; two receptor subtypes.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ State stabilisation over minutes to hours; circadian drift over the day; fast glutamate co-transmission in milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Orexin-neuron loss produces narcolepsy type 1 with cataplexy; orexin excess contributes to insomnia.


THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline the system is named sleep_wake_stabiliser.

It subscribes to metabolic_state, circadian_phase, sleep_pressure, and stress topics and publishes a state_stability parameter and an arousal_drive that the wakefulness_substrate and arousal_gain_broadcast pipelines require.

Its nearest engineering analogue is the hysteresis or Schmitt-trigger element added to a switch so that it latches cleanly into each state instead of chattering near the threshold.

The biological difference is that the stabiliser is itself a metabolic and circadian sensor, so the amount of hysteresis it supplies changes with hunger, stress, and time of day rather than being a fixed circuit constant.


ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).


CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).


WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A few tens of thousands of lateral hypothalamic cells making a peptide no other cell makes, which do not so much switch the brain awake as hold the switch steady, so that their loss is felt as a life of sleep and waking bleeding into each other.


PART SIX. THE HORMONAL AXES

This is the endocrine half of Layer 10, where the framework's dictum that a system is an ordered sequence of places carries over from wires to bloodstreams.

Each axis is a slow signalling loop that runs from a hypothalamic nucleus, through the pituitary gland, out to a peripheral organ, and back again as a returning hormone that reports on the result and closes a feedback thermostat.

Where the neural systems of the earlier parts trade in millisecond spikes over private cables, these axes trade in molecules released into shared blood, so their messages are slower, broadcast to every tissue at once, and read only by cells that carry the matching receptor.

The seven chapters here treat the stress axis, the reproduction axis, the thyroid set-point axis, the growth and repair axis, the melatonin darkness signal, the glucose fuel economy, and the gut-brain axis, and every one is tagged non-cognitive because it serves a regulatory, effector, or economic role rather than computing thoughts.

They are, together, the body's way of holding its internal conditions steady and of scheduling growth, reproduction, and repair across hours, days, and seasons.


CHAPTER 37. THE HYPOTHALAMIC-PITUITARY-ADRENAL (HPA) AXIS (The Body's Stress Thermostat) (NON-COG)

A near miss in traffic, a hard exam, a wound, or a cold morning all reach the same three-step chain that ends in a pulse of cortisol carried to every cell you own.

Stress names what trips it: any real or anticipated challenge to the body's steady state.

Thermostat names how it governs itself, because the hormone it releases loops back to switch off the very signal that summoned it.

Axis marks it as a hypothalamus-to-gland-to-body signalling line, not a place.

The hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, hereafter the HPA axis, is written out in full here on first use because the acronym recurs throughout this chapter.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The HPA axis is the body's central stress-response system, a hormonal cascade that converts a perceived challenge into a rise in the glucocorticoid hormone cortisol, a steroid that mobilises energy, tunes immunity, and reshapes attention.

A glucocorticoid is a steroid hormone made in the adrenal cortex that raises blood glucose and acts on nearly every tissue.

Its accomplishment is to match the body's readiness to the size and duration of a threat, and then, by negative feedback, to switch that readiness back off before it does harm.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The paraventricular nucleus of the hypothalamus, the small cluster of neurons that starts the cascade by releasing corticotropin-releasing hormone and arginine vasopressin into the pituitary portal blood.

• The anterior pituitary gland, whose corticotroph cells read that signal and secrete adrenocorticotropic hormone into the general circulation.

• The adrenal cortex, the outer rind of the adrenal gland sitting atop each kidney, whose cells synthesise and release cortisol on demand.

• The hippocampus and medial prefrontal cortex, section references in the Layer 9 manuscript's limbic sections, which sense circulating cortisol and mostly restrain the axis.

• The amygdala, which reads threat and mostly drives the axis on.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The first interface is the hypophysial portal system, a short private set of blood vessels running from the base of the hypothalamus down the pituitary stalk, carrying corticotropin-releasing hormone and arginine vasopressin as cargo in liquid format so that a tiny hypothalamic output reaches the pituitary undiluted.

The second interface is the open systemic bloodstream, into which the pituitary pours adrenocorticotropic hormone, addressed to the adrenal cortex.

The return path is also the systemic blood, carrying cortisol as a broadcast signal to every tissue and back to the brain's feedback sensors.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

At the hypothalamus the representation is a neural estimate of threat, a firing rate.

The portal hand-off converts that firing rate into a chemical concentration of corticotropin-releasing hormone, and arginine vasopressin adds gain by acting synergistically with it on the corticotroph.

The pituitary converts that concentration into a pulse of adrenocorticotropic hormone, amplifying one molecule of releasing hormone into many molecules of pituitary output.

The adrenal converts adrenocorticotropic hormone into cortisol, a second large amplification, so a small brain signal becomes a body-wide steroid tide.

The final transformation is interpretive: each target tissue reads the same cortisol level differently through its own receptors, turning one broadcast number into many local actions.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

The core motif is a negative-feedback control loop, the microcircuits catalogue's regulator pattern, with cortisol inhibiting both the pituitary and the hypothalamus.

A feedforward-with-delay motif between pituitary and adrenal generates the axis's characteristic pulses, because the loop's built-in time lags let it oscillate.

A gain-setting motif appears where arginine vasopressin multiplies the corticotroph's response to corticotropin-releasing hormone.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

The axis answers stress in two gears: adrenaline within seconds, and cortisol rising over 10 to 30 minutes and clearing over hours.

Beneath the responses runs the ultradian pulse of roughly one cortisol burst per hour, and over all of it the circadian programme with its dawn peak and midnight trough.

Feedback inhibition closes the loop on the tens-of-minutes timescale.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The axis secretes cortisol in ultradian pulses roughly once per hour, riding a circadian envelope that peaks near waking and falls to a trough late in the night.

An ultradian rhythm is one whose period is shorter than a day; a circadian rhythm is one whose period is about a day.

Sampling at ten-minute resolution shows each cortisol pulse trailing its adrenocorticotropic hormone pulse by a short delay of minutes, and total daily cortisol output is on the order of ten to twenty milligrams in an unstressed adult, with wide individual variation and moderate confidence.

The system responds in minutes, not milliseconds, and cannot represent fast events.

MODULATION AND GATING:

The main gate is glucocorticoid negative feedback acting fastest at the anterior pituitary and more slowly at the hypothalamus and hippocampus.

The axis is driven up by the amygdala and by inflammatory signals from the immune system, and restrained by the hippocampus and medial prefrontal cortex.

Circadian drive from the suprachiasmatic nucleus sets the daily envelope, so the same stressor lands on a different baseline at dawn than at midnight.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

Repeated or severe stress remodels the axis: chronic exposure can blunt or sensitise the response, alter glucocorticoid receptor numbers in the hippocampus, and shift the feedback set point.

Early-life stress can leave a long-lasting mark on axis reactivity through changes in receptor gene expression.

This is learning at the level of a control loop's parameters rather than of synaptic memory.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The hypothalamic and pituitary components differentiate in the embryo, and the adrenal cortex forms its steroid-making zones before and after birth.

A well-known developmental window of reduced stress reactivity appears in early postnatal life in mammals, when maternal care buffers the axis.

The feedback architecture matures gradually, reaching its adult set point through childhood and adolescence.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

A corticotropin-releasing hormone signalling system and a steroid stress response are ancient and conserved across vertebrates, with homologues reaching back to early chordates.

The three-tier hypothalamus-pituitary-adrenal arrangement is the vertebrate elaboration of a much older chemical stress signal.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

This aspect is the heart of the chapter, because the HPA axis is itself an endocrine system.

Its inputs are neural drive converted to corticotropin-releasing hormone and arginine vasopressin, plus immune cytokines that raise it.

Its outputs are adrenocorticotropic hormone and, above all, cortisol, which couples to essentially every other axis: it raises blood glucose in concert with the glucose axis, suppresses the reproductive and thyroid axes under chronic stress, and interacts with growth hormone.

Cortisol is thus both this axis's product and a master modulator of the others in this part.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Awake and active, the axis holds its circadian peak near rising and answers acute stressors with fast pulses.

In slow-wave sleep early in the night, cortisol sits at its daily trough.

Across the second half of sleep it climbs toward the morning peak, contributing to waking.

Under sustained stress the whole envelope is raised and its feedback loosened, the substrate for stress-related disease named in the Layer 11 states.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Too much cortisol is Cushing syndrome, with its truncal obesity, thin skin, and mood change.

Too little is Addison disease, with fatigue, low blood pressure, and salt craving, potentially fatal in an adrenal crisis.

Chronic dysregulation is implicated in depression, post-traumatic stress disorder, and metabolic syndrome.

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

Remove this axis and the body cannot mount a coordinated response to challenge; it fails to mobilise fuel, cannot restrain inflammation, and loses the daily cortisol rhythm that helps organise metabolism and waking.

The organism becomes fragile to any stressor, however mild.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Amygdala threat drive; hippocampal and prefrontal feedback; suprachiasmatic circadian drive; immune cytokines such as interleukin-6; blood cortisol at the feedback sensors.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Corticotropin-releasing hormone and arginine vasopressin into portal blood; adrenocorticotropic hormone into systemic blood; cortisol to all tissues.

• STATE.

◦ Current pulse phase; circadian envelope level; glucocorticoid receptor occupancy; feedback set point.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Pulse period about 60 minutes; circadian peak near waking; daily cortisol output on the order of 10 to 20 milligrams; adrenocorticotropic-to-cortisol delay of minutes.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Fast pituitary feedback seconds to minutes; slower hypothalamic feedback minutes to hours; circadian envelope 24 hours; set-point remodelling days to years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Cushing syndrome from excess cortisol; Addison disease from deficiency; flattened rhythm and impaired feedback in chronic stress and depression.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline this is stress_axis_governor, a control loop that subscribes to threat, circadian phase, and its own returning cortisol, and publishes a cortisol level to a body-wide topic every tissue can read.

Its nearest engineering analogue is a proportional-integral controller with transport delay and an oscillating output, the delays being what make it pulse.

The biological difference is that the controlled variable is a diffusing steroid read by receptor-bearing cells rather than a wire voltage, so every subscriber sees the same signal but responds by its own local rule.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A three-gland cascade whose product, cortisol, both mobilises the body for stress and loops back to shut its own cascade off, secreting in hourly pulses under a daily envelope that peaks at dawn.


CHAPTER 38. THE HYPOTHALAMIC-PITUITARY-GONADAL (HPG) AXIS (The Reproduction Clock) (NON-COG)

Deep in the arcuate nucleus a small population of neurons ticks like a metronome, and the tempo of that tick decides whether a body is a child or an adult, fertile or resting.

Reproduction names what it serves: puberty, cycling, and the making of eggs and sperm.

Clock names how it works, because its entire language is the frequency of a pulse, not its loudness.

Axis marks it as a hypothalamus-to-gland-to-body line.

The hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, hereafter the HPG axis, is spelled out in full here on first use because the acronym recurs throughout this chapter.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The HPG axis is the reproductive control system that governs sexual development, the menstrual and ovarian cycle, and the production of eggs and sperm.

It accomplishes reproduction by translating a pulsatile brain signal into the sex steroids and gametes that puberty and fertility require.

Its signature is that it encodes its commands in the frequency of hormone pulses, so the same molecules mean different things at different tempos.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The KNDy neurons of the arcuate nucleus of the hypothalamus, named for the peptides kisspeptin, neurokinin B, and dynorphin they co-express, which act as the pulse generator.

• The gonadotropin-releasing hormone neurons of the hypothalamus, driven by the KNDy generator to release their hormone in pulses.

• The anterior pituitary gland, whose gonadotroph cells read the pulses and secrete luteinising hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone.

• The gonads, the ovaries or testes, which make sex steroids such as oestradiol and testosterone and produce gametes.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The first interface is again the hypophysial portal system, carrying pulses of gonadotropin-releasing hormone from hypothalamus to pituitary.

The second is the systemic bloodstream, carrying luteinising hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone to the gonads.

The return path is systemic blood carrying oestradiol, testosterone, progesterone, and the peptide inhibin back to the hypothalamus and pituitary as feedback cargo.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

The representation begins as a pulse frequency set by the KNDy generator.

Gonadotropin-releasing hormone carries that frequency to the pituitary, where it is decoded: fast pulses favour luteinising hormone and slow pulses favour follicle-stimulating hormone, so a rate becomes a ratio of two output hormones.

The gonads convert that ratio into sex steroids and gametes, and the steroids then feed back, mostly as negative feedback but switching to positive feedback at mid-cycle to trigger the ovulatory surge.

That sign-switching feedback is the axis's most distinctive computation.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

The central motif is a frequency-coded oscillator, the microcircuits catalogue's pulse-generator pattern, built from mutually reinforcing and self-inhibiting KNDy peptides.

A frequency-decoder motif at the gonadotroph converts pulse rate into the luteinising-to-follicle-stimulating ratio.

A feedback motif that changes sign across the cycle turns steady braking into a single sharp trigger at ovulation.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

Gonadotropin-releasing hormone pulses every 60 to 120 minutes, the frequency itself encoding which gonadotropin dominates.

Over the pulses runs the roughly 28-day ovarian cycle, and over the lifespan the puberty switch-on and the menopausal switch-off - the widest temporal range of any axis.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

Gonadotropin-releasing hormone pulses occur roughly every 30 to 120 minutes, faster in early puberty at night and slowing by day.

The decoder can only read frequency within this band; too-continuous stimulation paradoxically shuts the axis down, the basis of drugs that use constant gonadotropin-releasing hormone to suppress it.

The full cycle in a human female runs about 28 days, so the axis spans timescales from an hour to a month.

MODULATION AND GATING:

Sex steroids gate the axis by feedback; energy status, stress, and sleep gate it too, which is why undernutrition or heavy stress can silence it.

Leptin, a fat-derived hormone, permits the axis to run only when energy stores suffice.

Cortisol from the stress axis suppresses it, coupling reproduction to adversity.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

The axis reorganises dramatically at puberty, when the pulse generator awakens, and again across pregnancy, lactation, and menopause.

These are programmed re-parameterisations of the loop rather than moment-to-moment learning.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

The gonadotropin-releasing hormone neurons famously migrate into the brain from the nasal placode during fetal life, and a failure of that migration causes a form of absent puberty.

The axis is briefly active in infancy, then quiet through childhood, then reawakened at puberty by rising KNDy drive.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Gonadotropin-releasing hormone is an ancient signalling peptide found across vertebrates and beyond, and the kisspeptin gatekeeper is likewise deeply conserved.

The frequency-coding trick is a vertebrate refinement of a very old reproductive signal.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

This aspect is the heart of the chapter.

Inputs are metabolic and stress hormones, chiefly leptin permitting and cortisol suppressing.

Outputs are the sex steroids, which act far beyond reproduction: on bone, muscle, brain, and mood, and which couple back to the growth axis at the pubertal growth spurt.

The axis is thus both a producer of reproductive hormones and a node wired to the metabolic and stress axes of this part.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

In early puberty the pulse generator runs faster at night during sleep, then slows by day.

Sleep, stress, and starvation all reconfigure the axis, and severe stress or energy deficit can suspend it entirely, a state named in the Layer 11 schedule.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Failure of gonadotropin-releasing hormone neurons to migrate gives Kallmann syndrome, absent puberty with an absent sense of smell.

Polycystic ovary syndrome involves altered pulse frequency and androgen excess.

Loss of the axis with age drives menopause and the male decline in testosterone.

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

Remove this axis and the body never enters puberty, never cycles, and makes no gametes; the sex steroids that shape the skeleton, musculature, and brain are missing.

The organism remains reproductively a child.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Metabolic signals such as leptin; stress cortisol; circadian and sleep drive; sex-steroid and inhibin feedback.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Gonadotropin-releasing hormone pulses; luteinising hormone and follicle-stimulating hormone; sex steroids and gametes from the gonads.

• STATE.

◦ Pulse frequency; cycle phase; feedback sign (negative most of the cycle, positive at the ovulatory surge).

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Pulse interval 30 to 120 minutes; fast pulses favour luteinising hormone, slow favour follicle-stimulating hormone; human cycle about 28 days.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Pulse interval tens of minutes to two hours; menstrual cycle about one month; developmental reawakening across years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Kallmann syndrome and hypogonadotropic hypogonadism; polycystic ovary syndrome; hypothalamic amenorrhoea from stress or low energy.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline this is reproduction_pulse_axis, whose signature is that meaning lives in message rate, not amplitude.

Its nearest engineering analogue is a frequency-modulated transmitter feeding a frequency decoder, with a downstream stage that reads pulse rate to choose between two outputs.

The biological difference is that overly steady input jams the decoder, so the channel only works when it pulses, the opposite of most engineered links.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A reproductive axis that speaks entirely in pulse frequency, where fast ticks call for one gonadotropin and slow ticks for the other, and where steady stimulation shuts the whole thing down.


CHAPTER 39. THE HYPOTHALAMIC-PITUITARY-THYROID (HPT) AXIS (The Metabolic Set-Point Dial) (NON-COG)

Turn this dial up and every cell in the body burns a little hotter and faster; turn it down and the whole organism idles, cools, and slows.

Metabolic names what it governs: the basal rate at which the body spends energy.

Set-point names how it works, because it defends a target level of thyroid hormone as a thermostat defends a temperature.

Dial names that its output is a slowly adjusted level, not a pulse or a spike.

The hypothalamic-pituitary-thyroid axis, hereafter the HPT axis, is written out in full here on first use because the acronym recurs throughout this chapter.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

The HPT axis sets the body's metabolic set point, the resting speed of energy use, by regulating the blood level of thyroid hormone.

It accomplishes long-term metabolic stability: it holds thyroid hormone within a narrow band so that basal metabolic rate, thermogenesis, growth, and brain development proceed at the right pace.

It is the slowest of the great dials, adjusting over days and weeks.

THE CHAIN OF STATIONS (THE LAYER 9 ROSTER):

• The paraventricular nucleus of the hypothalamus, whose neurons release thyrotropin-releasing hormone.

• The anterior pituitary gland, whose thyrotroph cells secrete thyroid-stimulating hormone, also called thyrotropin.

• The thyroid gland in the neck, which makes the hormones thyroxine, called T4, and triiodothyronine, called T3.

• Peripheral tissues and the brain's feedback sensors, which read circulating thyroid hormone and adjust the axis.

THE PATHWAYS AND INTERFACES:

The first interface is the hypophysial portal system, carrying thyrotropin-releasing hormone to the pituitary.

The second is the systemic bloodstream, carrying thyroid-stimulating hormone to the thyroid.

The return path is systemic blood carrying T4 and T3 back to the pituitary and hypothalamus, where they exert negative feedback.

THE TRANSFORMATION ALONG THE CHAIN:

The representation is a target metabolic level.

Thyrotropin-releasing hormone sets the pituitary's sensitivity; the pituitary secretes thyroid-stimulating hormone in proportion to how far below target the current thyroid hormone level sits.

The thyroid converts thyroid-stimulating hormone into secreted T4, most of it a prohormone.

Peripheral tissues then convert T4 into the more active T3 by removing one iodine atom, so the final active signal is set partly in the target tissues themselves, a distributed final transformation unusual among the axes.

THE MOTIFS IT RUNS (THE LAYER 8 INVENTORY):

The dominant motif is a high-gain negative-feedback loop, the microcircuits catalogue's set-point regulator, so sensitive that thyroid-stimulating hormone changes logarithmically for small changes in thyroid hormone.

A local conversion motif in peripheral tissues, controlled by deiodinase enzymes, lets each tissue tune its own active hormone level from the shared prohormone.

TEMPORAL SIGNATURE:

The slowest of the great dials: thyroid-stimulating hormone shows a nocturnal rise, thyroxine's half-life is about seven days, and set-point changes play out over weeks - this axis does not do minutes.

CAPACITY AND LIMITS:

The axis is slow: thyroid hormone has a long half-life, T4 living about a week in blood, so the whole loop responds over days.

Thyroid-stimulating hormone shows a modest circadian variation with a peak in the night.

The set point is individually tight, each person defending their own narrow thyroid-stimulating hormone range, which is why a value normal for the population can be abnormal for one person.

MODULATION AND GATING:

Cold exposure raises the axis to generate heat; severe illness and starvation lower it, sparing energy, in the state called non-thyroidal illness.

Cortisol and other signals modulate the local deiodinase conversion, gating how much active hormone each tissue makes.

PLASTICITY AND LEARNING:

The set point can reset over months, for example after pregnancy or with autoimmune disease.

This is slow parameter drift rather than synaptic learning.

DEVELOPMENT AND ASSEMBLY:

Thyroid hormone is essential for fetal and infant brain development, and its severe deficiency in early life causes irreversible intellectual disability, historically called cretinism, which is why newborns are screened for it.

The axis and its feedback mature through fetal and early postnatal life.

EVOLUTIONARY PROVENANCE:

Thyroid hormone signalling is ancient, predating vertebrates, and its role in metamorphosis in amphibians hints at its deep function as a developmental and metabolic timer.

THE ENDOCRINE COUPLING:

This aspect is the heart of the chapter.

Inputs are cold, energy status, and cortisol, which modulate the axis.

Outputs are T4 and T3, which set the metabolic tempo of every tissue and interact with the growth axis, potentiating growth hormone's actions, and with the reproductive axis.

The thyroid dial thus sets the background rate against which the other axes act.

STATES AND THE LAYER 11 SCHEDULE:

Thyroid-stimulating hormone peaks at night and dips by day, a gentle rhythm rather than a sharp one.

In prolonged critical illness the whole axis is suppressed, a distinct low-metabolic state noted in the Layer 11 schedule.

DISEASES AND DISORDERS:

Too much thyroid hormone is hyperthyroidism, classically Graves disease, with heat intolerance, weight loss, racing heart, and anxiety.

Too little is hypothyroidism, often Hashimoto disease, with cold intolerance, weight gain, fatigue, and slowed thought.

HOW IT COLOURS THE ORGANISM:

Remove this axis and the organism loses its metabolic thermostat: without thyroid hormone, basal rate collapses, body temperature falls, thought and movement slow, and in early life the brain fails to develop.

The whole body's tempo is this dial's setting.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• SUBSCRIBES (INPUTS).

◦ Cold and thermoregulatory drive; energy status; cortisol; thyroid hormone feedback at pituitary and hypothalamus.

• PUBLISHES (OUTPUTS).

◦ Thyrotropin-releasing hormone; thyroid-stimulating hormone; thyroxine (T4) and triiodothyronine (T3).

• STATE.

◦ Current thyroid hormone level; individual set point; tissue deiodinase activity.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ T4 half-life about one week; thyroid-stimulating hormone night peak; a logarithmic thyroid-stimulating-hormone response to thyroid hormone.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Loop response over days; set-point drift over months; developmental role across fetal and infant life.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Graves disease (hyperthyroidism); Hashimoto disease (hypothyroidism); congenital deficiency causing developmental disability; non-thyroidal illness syndrome.

THE ENGINEERING NOTE:

As a engineering pipeline this is metabolic_setpoint_dial, a slow high-gain regulator that holds a target hormone level and lets each subscriber locally sharpen the active dose.

Its nearest engineering analogue is a thermostat with a very long time constant plus per-room trim valves.

The biological difference is that the final active signal is manufactured inside each target tissue by deiodinase enzymes, so the setpoint is defended centrally but realised locally.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

CODE SAMPLE:

This section does not apply to this system for the reason that this system serves an effector, relay, regulatory, or economic role rather than a cognitive-computational role, and is tagged (NON-COG).

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The slowest axis, defending an individual set point of thyroid hormone with logarithmic sensitivity, whose active form is finished not in the gland but inside each target tissue.


CHAPTER 40. THE GROWTH HORMONE AND SOMATOSTATIN AXIS (The Night-Repair Axis) (NON-COG)

The largest burst of growth hormone you make each day arrives not in daylight effort but in the deepest trough of your first deep sleep.

Growth names what it builds: bone, muscle, and the repair of tissue.

Somatostatin names its brake, the inhibitory partner that carves the smooth drive into sharp pulses.

Night-repair names when and why it works, in step with slow-wave sleep.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT ACCOMPLISHES:

This axis is the growth and tissue-repair system, releasing growth hormone in nightly pulses that drive linear growth in childhood and tissue maintenance throughout life.

It accomplishes growth and repair by pairing a releasing signal with an inhibiting one, so that its output is not a steady level but a train of clean pulses timed to sleep.
